Supporting Texts
Text S1 The urgency of exploring global phosphorus sustainable
pathways
Our planet has a history of approximately 4.5 billion years, yet it has been less
than a century since we began mining phosphate rock. The Anthropocene era is
characterized by profound human-induced disruption to the global geochemical cycles
1. In particular, the global phosphorus (P) pattern has undergone unprecedented
changes in time and space. Agricultural production and the trade of farming products
drive most of the global P flow. Over 90% of the mined P is used for fertilizer
production 2,3, while mineral P fertilizers and agricultural product trade influence the
global P cycle 4-6. This process shifts P resources in agricultural products and reduces
local opportunities for P recycling 5,7. The conflict between food security and P input
lies in the scarcity of P resources and its detrimental environmental impact 8, with
inefficient management exacerbating P shortages and pollution under intensive trade.
These challenges underscore the need to address P sustainability, particularly to
reassess how human socioeconomic development can establish effective P
sustainability strategies, including the rational use of P resources and mitigating or
even halting further ecological damage caused by P.
Sustainable management for P at the national level faces numerous issues. In
industrialized nations, disproportionate use of fertilizers and manure results in low P
use efficiency (PUE) 9,10. A large amount of excess P is retained in the soil, increasing
P saturation and reducing crop P availability. Many developing countries are still in

=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
4
the early stages of agricultural development, with low P fertilizer application and crop
yields. In contrast, Brazil, the UK, China, and India are currently in the phase of P
surplus with low nutrient use efficiency 10-12. Inefficient P use and management have
disrupted the natural P cycle and made P one of the major contributors to exceeding
planetary boundaries 7. This increasing and globally imbalanced use of non-renewable
resources could deplete P reserves within 100 to 150 years 10. The widespread use of
P fertilizers and the poor management of human and animal manure pose threats to
SDG6 (Clean Water and Sanitation) and SDG14 (Life Below Water), as P losses from
farmland due to surface runoff led to freshwater and coastal eutrophication 5,13. The
disorganized P flow interactions in socio-economic systems and inefficient P
management present severe obstacles to achieving SDG2.3, aiming to double
smallholder farms' productivity. According to Langhans et al. 14, five major regions
must increase P fertilizer use by 39% (126 Tg) between 2015 and 2030 to meet this
goal. However, given the excessive P damage to water bodies, the surface water
systems can already handle the growing P pressure from agriculture. Sustainable P
management will play a vital role in achieving SDG2 (Zero Hunger) 14.
The use of mineral P fertilizers has significantly increased in the past 70 years,
providing food for billions of people and livestock 15. However, 1 in 7 farmers cannot
afford fertilizers, affecting productivity 16. In Africa, insufficient P fertilizer use may
lead to a 30% reduction in crop yields by 2050. Meanwhile, Europe, North America,
and Southeast Asia face water quality threats from excessive P fertilizer use 17. Global
P losses from land to freshwater have doubled over the past century, with an

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
5
estimated 9 to 14 million tons lost annually, threatening ecosystems and human health,
costing $2 billion annually in the U.S. alone 13,18. Freshwater aquaculture meets the
demand for 20% of animal protein for 3 billion people, but increased P input is
causing direct pollution to aquatic ecosystems 19. Eighty-five percent of the global
phosphate rock reserves are concentrated in five countries—Morocco, China, Russia,
Egypt, and Tunisia, with 70% located in Morocco and Western Sahara. Most
countries rely on imported P fertilizers, making them vulnerable to supply risks 20.
Although reserves could support over 100 years of mining and potentially increase
new P reserves, economic, geopolitical, and policy factors could limit availability 21,22.
For example, in 2008, phosphate rock prices spiked by 800%, leading to a fertilizer
price surge that severely impacted the livelihoods of the world’s poorest farmers,
highlighting the vulnerability of P supply 22. Climate change is exacerbating P losses,
increasing the risk of algal blooms and coastal dead zones while altering the carbon
cycle. P pollution increases methane emissions from freshwater ecosystems and stores
organic carbon in lakebed sediments. A recent study projects that increased P losses
will raise methane emissions from lakes and reservoirs globally by up to 30% of
current CO2 emissions from fossil fuels over the next century 23.
Unsustainable P use is pushing food security further from reach while leaving
behind polluted freshwater legacies, many of which are beyond ecological restoration.
Ten years have passed since the global anthropogenic P flow was identified as
exceeding its planetary boundary. In 2013, the opportunity was highlighted to achieve
a 20% improvement in nutrient use efficiency across the entire food and waste system

=== PDF PAGE 7 ===
6
by 2020. The Post-2020 Global Biodiversity Framework working group proposed
reducing excess nutrient pollution by 50% by 2030. However, P management remains
largely ignored in the food and environmental policy agendas of most countries and
international conventions. Progress remains hindered by a lack of policy and public
awareness, fragmentation of actions and policies, and the absence of
intergovernmental coordination. Numerous studies have clearly revealed the global
and interconnected nature of the P problem: it is not a burden or liability that can be
exported to other geopolitical regions or stakeholders; it is everyone’s responsibility,
and solutions will require participation from diverse actors and multi-level actions.
We are also reminded that, although global-scale models produce interesting macro
analyses, the challenge lies in linking these results to local solutions. Such studies aim
to inspire the international community to create opportunities for change and manage
the P cycle. Ultimately, to implement the necessary changes, it seems there is an
opportunity for more effective P governance at all levels, from global to local. We
need to mobilize many disciplines to navigate this global system and invest in
evidence-based, appropriate, and fit-for-purpose solutions. To achieve this, we must
find systematic approaches to establishing new research systems and pathways,
integrating this thinking with other major nutrient cycles (such as carbon). In 2030,
the global P issue will face a true turning point, and the insights provided by this
research offer an opportunity to improve global P governance for sustainability.
Text S2 Data source and access
In this study, the data can be categorized into two types: activity data and

=== PDF PAGE 8 ===
7
coefficient data. The data were primarily derived from official statistical records and
consisted of P-related activity data, including mining and the production and trade of
P-related products, and calculation coefficients. Metadata for all products were
obtained from official international and national databases. Calculated parameters
(e.g., P content of products) were obtained from the published literature and official
reports. A detailed description of the dataset types, structure, sources, and usage
instructions is provided in Text S2, Table S2-S4, and the Data Availability section.
The limitations of the P substance flow analysis (P_SFA) model, as well as the
procedures and outcomes of the uncertainty and sensitivity analyses, are detailed in
the Text S10, Tables S11-S12, and Figures S19-S24.
Not all agricultural products have the same P concentration, making the use of
biomass flows as an indicator of trade's impact on global P cycling inaccurate. For
instance, certain products with large global trade volumes (e.g., palm oil) have very
low P content 5. In other words, converting all biomass flows into a single unit is
essential for estimating the impact of global trade on the global P cycle. We
calculated P flows in all considered products (including trade) by multiplying the
weight of P-containing products and the trade volumes (imports and exports) between
countries by their respective P concentrations (Food Standards Agency, 2002).
According to studies on anthropogenic P cycling (Table S1), the FAOSTAT
database (Food and Agriculture Organization, FAO) despite being incomplete,
provides the most comprehensive agricultural product (exchange) data currently
available for depicting global P flow scenarios 5. We obtained production and trade

=== PDF PAGE 9 ===
8
quantities for 460 agricultural products from FAOSTAT’s agriculture production and
trade modules (https://www.fao.org/faostat/zh/#data, accessed December 2024).
Additionally, FAOSTAT’s trade module provides total import and export data for
crops and livestock products in each FAO country/region (n=245) between 1961 and
2022, along with networks of bilateral trade relationships.
The trade data used in this study excludes food aid and does not fully account for
re-exported goods, as these are generally reported as negligible 5. The P content of the
460 agricultural products considered in this study and related data sources are detailed
in Table S1. All data processing was performed using Python, and the availability of
the code is provided in the manuscript.
Phosphate rock reserves and mining data were sourced from the US Geological
Survey (USGS,
https://www.usgs.gov/centers/national-minerals-information-center/rare-earths-statisti
cs-and-information). Mineral phosphate fertilizer usage data was obtained from
FAOSTAT, while production data for non-fertilizer products was derived from their
relative proportion in chemical fertilizer production. Mineral phosphate fertilizer trade
data (including MAP, DAP, TSP, phosphoric acid, and phosphate rock) was obtained
from The United Nations Comtrade database
(https://comtradeplus.un.org/TradeFlow).
We also compared annual P flows in globally traded crops with the P content in
global crop production and domestic mineral phosphate fertilizer usage in various
countries. This data was sourced from FAOSTAT’s “Production” module (see

=== PDF PAGE 10 ===
9
FAOSTAT). Data on arable land and permanent crop areas was also obtained from
FAOSTAT’s “Inputs” module (2022 data).
For the computation of the different P flows, P concentration data and
information on the number of different goods had to be compiled. In many cases, it
was possible to obtain the relevant data from the Office of Regional Statistics or other
governmental authorities. Collaborations with private companies were often difficult,
as the necessary information would have violated business secrets. If possible, all
categories and subcategories provided in the obtained data were accounted for. In
cases where this was not possible, P concentration values of the closest related
category were chosen. In most cases, P flows were calculated by applying the
following equation (Text S5, Eq 5-1). In cases, where no data for a flow was found in
literature or statistics, the principle of mass conservation was applied to derive a
missing outflow, for example, by calculating the difference between all known input
and output flows according to the equation (Text S5, Eq 5-3, 4, 5).
To determine the balance of a stock (process), likewise, the difference between
all input and output flows was computed. In the global P substance flow analysis
(P_SFA) model, stocks are represented by area-related processes such as natural
vegetation soils, agricultural soils, urban soils, and landfills. No reconciliation of flow
and stock data was needed as values did not contradict due to the application of
balancing flows and annual stock changes). All balancing flows were net in-or export
flows that else could not have been determined, since respective statistical data at the
regional level was not available.

=== PDF PAGE 11 ===
10
Some flows were neglected due to their minor quantitative relevance or had to be
excluded, as no data was available. For example, P flows through some wood in waste,
paper, cardboard, textile, and sanitary textile fractions were not considered, as either
their statistics were not available, not sufficiently differentiated, or their fate was not
clearly traceable. Also, as in the case of paper and cardboard, textiles, and sanitary
textiles, fractions can be expected to be low to very low 24. Losses by wind erosion
from agricultural soils, on the other hand, are likely to be relevant, but current
knowledge of this process allows quantification only on a small scale in specific sites
25. Similarly, weathering was not included in the flow chart (Figures S1-S4), as the
information on these flows was not sufficient. Due to the diversity of fertilizer types,
we have only considered P-related fertilizers, including P fertilizers and compound
fertilizers.
Finally, data from published literature are also used for complementing and
comparison to check coefficients. We selected literature based on the following
criteria: (1) Studies based on observation data in the global are selected in priority; (2)
Studies with the methodology scientifically described are included in priority; (3)
Studies published in highly-cited journals are included in priority. (4) Studies
conducted by well-known research institutions were included as a priority.
The coefficients used in the global P_SFA model are listed in Tables S2-S4. In
general, the accuracy (three categories of high, moderate, and low reliabilities) and
source classification of the data were determined according to the following criteria to

=== PDF PAGE 12 ===
11
guarantee our confidence in these parameters values: qualitatively (inclusion and
exclusion criteria based on this research team’s previous research) 8,26.
1) Data from our field research were included before citation data;
2) Data from the studies based on observation data were included in priority;
3) Dates from the studies with the methodology scientifically described were
included in priority;
4) Data from the studies published in highly-cited journals were included in
priority;
5) Data from the studies conducted by well-known research institutions were
included in priority.
Text S3 Improved multi-year global-scale P_SFA model
A large body of research at various geographic scales has shown that
quantitatively tracking P flow trajectories within complex societal systems and
identifying links between P pollution across different sectors are crucial for
identifying major P sources, flow pathways, and P use efficiency 8,26-28. Visualizing P
stocks and flows has enhanced the understanding of how human activities drive
societal P consumption and pollution mechanisms. However, due to the complexity of
systems and the lack of a standardized research framework, global related research
remains fragmented, and quantification models lack consistency and generalizability.
We have previously developed a watershed P flow analysis model (WPSFA
model)8,26,29, based on which we built a P flow network for both the social system and
natural ecosystem at the watershed scale and proposed the feasibility of using WPSFA

=== PDF PAGE 13 ===
12
to enhance the understanding of the global P cycle. WPSFA covers the entire process
from mining, production, and processing to consumption, disposal, and emission. On
this basis, we further developed a tracking framework for dynamic global P cycle
processes, where the P flow network connects multiple social production subsystems,
including mining systems, chemical industry systems, crop systems, livestock systems,
aquaculture, forestry, trade, residential consumption systems, waste disposal systems,
recycling systems, and natural (soil and water) systems. Notably, this is, to our
knowledge, the first attempt to incorporate global trade and P footprint analysis (PFA)
concepts into the global flow and environmental (re)distribution of P from a whole
lifecycle perspective, which is critical, as the role of trade in driving the P cycle can
no longer be ignored. Further, we specifically focus on the food processing system
and treat it as an independent P flow transfer station (which is divided into primary
and secondary processing) rather than overlooking or hiding it within the livestock
and consumption systems. The amount of P loss during product processing is
staggering. These improvements will help us select appropriate strategies to optimize
the P resource use structure and control P pollution. Detailed model hierarchy is
referenced in Text S4 and Figures S1-S4.
Text S4 Hierarchy of the global P_SFA model for anthropogenic P
cycling
The anthropogenic P cycling globally consists of P flows through the different
social-economic systems and environmental media. Population growth and its demand
for food have accelerated the flow of P by producing phosphate ores into various

=== PDF PAGE 14 ===
13
P-related chemicals that sustain the entire ecosystem. The anthropogenic P cycle
begins when P is extracted from P minerals and enters the social system and ends
when P-containing commodities are exported directly to the watershed or accumulate
in cultivated water, or uncultivated land 30.
The conceptual framework of the dynamic global P_SFA model is shown in
Figures S1-S4. Based on the life-cycle of P flow, systems in the watershed (nature)
and social are divided into eight subsystems: mining, fabrication, production,
processing, consumption, trade, disposal, and emission (Figures S2 and S3) 30,31.
Specifically, as a dynamic SFA, reserves are defined as the total amount of P stored,
accumulated, or consumed in various subprocesses. Overall, more than 70 flows and
10 reserves were calculated to depict the global P flow landscape comprehensively.
External P mineral resource and product imports first go through fabrication and
production and then are transferred to consumption for consumption or exported
directly out of the local social systems, and then to disposal for waste treatment and
finally accumulated in life-support. However, P accumulated in social systems also
comes from significant P losses from fabrication, production, and consumption 30. We
further identify 10 major compartments based on previous research (Figures S2 and
S3) 30,32,33. The fabrication includes mining ( PMSP ) and chemical industry ( CISP ). The
production includes crop farming ( ACSP ), animal husbandry ( LBSP ), including scale
breeding (
1
LBSP ) and domestic breeding ( 2LBSP ) and food processing system ( FPSP ),
which includes primary processing (
1
FPSP ) and secondary processing ( 2FPSP ). The
consumption system ( RCSP ) includes urban consumption ( 1RCSP ) and rural

=== PDF PAGE 15 ===
14
consumption (
2
RCSP ). The products trade system ( PTSP ) includes phosphate rock raw
materials, fertilizers, and all P-containing agricultural products. The disposal ( WTSP )
provides wastewater treatment ( wastewaterP ) and solid waste disposal (  solid wasteP ). The P
emissions system (PES) includes uncultivated land ( _non croplandP ), cultivated land
( croplandP ), and surface water ( SWP ). The exchanger consists of imports/exports. Based
on the previous study by Chen and Graedel 13, the coupled model identified 10 waste
streams in the P lifecycle, ranging from phosphate rock mining to P consumption.
These include on-site slag waste, primary phosphate rock slurry (PMS), phosphoric
acid tailings, secondary PMS, phosphogypsum, P slag, crop residues, animal waste,
human excreta, and food waste.
Mining ( PMSP ) is the primary consumption activities and extraction activity of
phosphate ores. However, those P contained in other mineral resources, such as fossil
fuels and metals, where P is regarded as impurities. The P inflow in this compartment
is phosphate rocks from the lithosphere. The P outflows include natural phosphate
rocks used in the P chemical industry or exported to other regions, and gangues lost to
uncultivated land. Global phosphate rock reserves and mineable phosphate rock,
expressed as elemental P, are calculated based on global phosphate rock reserve and
mining production data reported in the USGS Mineral Statistics and Information
(https://www.usgs.gov/centers/national-minerals-information-center/rare-earths-statist
ics-and-information). It is assumed that the P₂O₅ content of phosphate rock reserves is
28%, and the P₂O₅ content of phosphate rock concentrates is 31% (see Tables S2-S4).

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
15
Mining waste is estimated based on the volume of phosphate rock mined, production
loss coefficients, and the P content of each type of waste.
P chemical industry ( CISP ) means the processing activity of phosphate rocks,
including the fertilizer industry ( CIS ACSP ), detergent industry ( CIS RCSP ), pesticide
industry ( CIS ACSP ), and feed industry ( CIS LBSP ). In this compartment, the P inflow is
phosphate rocks ( PMS CISP ) and element P from the compartment PMSP . The P
outflows include various P chemical products used in crop farming ( ACSP ), animal
husbandry ( LBSP ), human consumption ( RCSP ) or exported, wastewater emitted to
surface water ( SWP ) or to wastewater treatment system ( WTSP ) for further treatment,
and solid waste emitted to uncultivated land ( _non croplandP ) or to solid waste treatment
system (  solid wasteP ). Industrial and domestic wastewater entering the sewage treatment
plant are discharged after meeting the standards through treatment.
Crop farming ( ACSP ) here refers to the cultivation activities in the cultivated land.
Crop categories are listed in Table S2. The P inflows include fertilizer and pesticide
coming from the P chemical industry or imported from other regions, seed,
atmospheric deposition, crop straw return to cultivated land, human excreta, livestock
excreta, and feed grain. The P outflows include primary crop product to the food/feed
industry (uptake by crops), P removal in crop residues, P removal through mowing,
losses through runoff and erosion, and losses through leaching. The difference
between inputs and outputs is considered as the change in reserves within agricultural
soils.

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
16
According to the research strategy of Chen and Graedel 13, the use of recycled P
as organic fertilizer is typically calculated by multiplying the total output of each
organic P resource (including animal waste, crop residues, urban human excreta, and
rural human excreta) by the corresponding recycling rate. However, there are
significant differences in recycling rates between regions due to variations in factors
such as farmers' income, fertilizer access, farming habits, management practices,
technological levels, and local labor costs. Therefore, estimating how much organic P
resource is used as organic fertilizer is challenging. Based on the limited information
provided in the literature, we analyzed the statistical characteristics of empirical data.
We used the median to specify the quantity of organic P resources recycled as organic
fertilizer. For rates that show significant temporal variation in the literature, we
derived five values for the periods of 1961–1970, 1971–1980, 1981–1990, 1991–2000,
2001–2010, and 2011-2021. In cases where no significant time variation was
observed, we used a single value for the period from 1961 to 2021. We applied a
similar approach to atmospheric P deposition rates, P loss rates from runoff,
agricultural land erosion to water bodies, and P leaching from agricultural land.
Stockbreeding system ( LBSP ) refers to the breeding of terrestrial animals (pig,
cattle, sheep, and poultry), cultivating freshwater products, and mariculture products
(animals products list in Table S2) (this study calculated the P content of aquatic
products only in the sale link of animal products because the usability of the data, and
did not calculate the P content of aquatic products in stock). The P inflows are feed
produced locally or imported from other regions, fodder from uncultivated land, and

=== PDF PAGE 18 ===
17
food residue from human consumption. The P outflows include live animals to the
food/feed industry, food processing and animal excreta recycled to cultivated land or
lost to surface water.
Food processing ( FPSP ) includes two major activities: primary processing ( 1FPSP )
and secondary processing (
2
FPSP ). This study includes the first stage of processing of
crops (husking, flouring, etc.) and animals (slaughtering). However, the higher
processing stages are excluded due to the unavailability of production details for
various forms of processed products. The P inflows of the food industry include the
part of primary crop products from crop farming used as food and live animals from
animal husbandry. The P outflows include crop food, animal food, processing wastes
of crop products and live animals, and wastewater emitted to surface water or
wastewater treatment systems for further treatment. As a high-quality feed source, the
processing wastes of crop products will enter the feed industry for further treatment.
The processed wastes of live animals, mainly referred to as bone, fur, and blood, are
abandoned in landfills or other uncultivated land. The P inflows into the feed industry
are primary crop products used as feed (feed grain and bran), processing wastes of
crop products, and feed imported from other regions. It is worth noting that in the
model framework, the P flow calculated by the food processing subsystem only
represents the amount of P lost in the process of food processing, the input represents
the P lost in the processing of crop products and animal products, and the output
represents the destination of the P lost in processing.

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
18
Consumption system ( RCSP ), including urban consumption ( 1RCSP ) and rural
consumption (
2
RCSP ) contains a group of human activities. The main P inflows are
food from the food industry or imported from other regions and synthetic detergent
from the P chemical industry or imported from other regions. The P outflows include
wastewater, solid waste, and human excreta that is recycled to cultivated land or
emitted to surface water or wastewater treatment systems.
The trading system ( PTSP ) includes the cross-regional flow and direction of P in
global P product trade. This is calculated annually by summing the P flow in all
imported phosphate ores, phosphate fertilizers, crops, and livestock products,
covering data from 1961 to 2022 (with phosphate ore and fertilizer data starting from
1986). The calculated P flow from agricultural products is divided into eight
categories, with detailed product descriptions provided in Table S2. To estimate the
contribution of trade to the displacement of P resources, we compared the global P
flow with the global use of mineral P fertilizers and the global trade volume of
mineral P fertilizers. Additionally, to estimate the P flow between agricultural product
trade partners, we summarized the P content in specific crops and livestock products
imported by each country, calculated the P flow for each trade partner, and described
the quantity and direction of these flows.
Wastewater treatment ( WTSP ) represents the human activity removing
contaminants from wastewater ( wastewaterP ) and solid waste disposal (  solid wasteP ) is the
compartment for treating solid waste from the P chemical industry, animal husbandry,
and human consumption. We used food waste data collected from the FAO database

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
19
(www.fao.org/faostat/en/#data/FBSH) (FAOSTAT, 2022) and calculated the P loss
caused by per capita food waste using the method provided by Oita et al. 34. For waste
from extraction and industrial production, we assumed a runoff coefficient of 5% 13
flowing into water bodies. Based on these empirical or assumed rates, we estimated
the atmospheric P deposition, P loss from agricultural land runoff and erosion into
water bodies, P leaching from agricultural land, and P runoff from mining and
industrial waste.
Given the complexity of global P cycling and the practical considerations of
model operability, this study established specific system boundaries for the flows of
P-containing waste (such as animal manure, human excreta, and food residues) when
constructing the P substance flow analysis model. Specifically, the model assumes
that P derived from these wastes is primarily managed and recycled at the national or
regional level and does not participate in international trade flows. This assumption is
based on several considerations. First, unlike commodities such as phosphate rock,
chemical fertilizers, and agricultural products, the international trade volume of
P-containing wastes is minimal worldwide. Its scale is almost negligible compared
with other forms of P flow. In existing national and global P-SFA model frameworks,
it is also uncommon to include waste-derived P in international trade flows. Second,
the cross-border transport and trade of P-containing wastes face substantial challenges,
including regulatory constraints, economic feasibility (e.g., high transportation costs
and differences in treatment standards), and infrastructure requirements (including
collection, processing, storage, and transportation systems). These factors mean that

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
20
the international trade system for waste-derived P remains far from mature or widely
adopted. Moreover, given the limited availability of global trade data for such
non-standardized materials, incorporating them into the model would introduce
excessive uncertainty. Therefore, within the modeling framework, the recovery,
utilization, and discharge management of waste-derived P are considered exclusively
within the study region (country or region).
Uncultivated land ( _non croplandP ) refers to the terrestrial land of watersheds that
cannot be used for crop farming, such as forest, grassland, construction land, and
unused land.
Surface water ( SWP ) is any water body located within the watershed boundary,
including all ditch, stream, river, lake, and underwater bed sediment in the watershed.
Import/export refers to the exchange of P-associated products with other regions.
Note the downstream utilization of exported products and the upstream process of
imported products.
The P-SFA model developed in this study adopts a fixed structure from a
methodological perspective to strictly comply with the law of mass conservation and
ensure the traceability of elemental flows. Within the SFA framework, once the model
structure is established, the upstream and downstream relationships (that is, the input–
output relationships among subsystems) are explicitly defined, reflecting the
predetermined pathways through which P moves within the system. Therefore, the
model structure is considered relatively fixed to accurately characterize the mass
balance of P under given conditions. This fixed structure is a fundamental feature of

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
21
the SFA methodology, ensuring the precise quantification of detailed P flows. It is
also consistent with the relatively mature global P-SFA modeling frameworks,
thereby enhancing the comparability of the results.
It is important to clarify that although the construction of P-SFA models is
highly mature and global-scale P-SFA models have been applied, certain influential
factors are often overlooked or not explicitly addressed in many studies.
Fixed-structure P-SFA models do not expressly incorporate complex socio-economic
feedback mechanisms, behavioral responses, policy lags, or regulatory delays.
Integrating these nonlinear, strongly time-lagged social dynamics into global-scale
P-SFA modeling poses methodological and data-related challenges that exceed the
scope and primary objectives of the relevant research. Future studies will seek to
explore more effective approaches to integrating these dynamic feedback mechanisms
to provide more comprehensive insights.
Text S5 Calculation methods
As illustrated in Figures S1-S3, we constructed a complex socioeconomic system
P-interaction network on a horizontal scale. This network encompasses various
components including geological P reserves (PR), phosphate mining system (PMS), P
chemical industrial system (CIS), agriculture crop system (ACS), livestock breeding
system (LBS), Aquaculture system (AS), forestry system (FS), food processing
system (FPS), residential consumption system (RCS), waste treatment system (WTS),
and P emissions system (PES). The model also accounts for the impact of import and
export trade at the national level on the global P cycle and the balance based on these

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
22
processes (the detailed P_SFA model structure and processes can be found in Texts
S3-S5). On a vertical scale, the upper boundary of the study area is defined as
approximately 1 km above the Earth's surface, primarily focusing on atmospheric
deposition rather than atmospheric circulation 9. The lower boundary is the Earth's
crust, which includes mineral resources, soil deposits, and precipitation runoff but
excludes deep groundwater. The timescale for this study spans 1961 to 2021.
Historical P stocks and flows were calculated using a combined top-down and
bottom-up approach, driven by production.
The global P_SFA model is based on SFA, and its core is the principle of mass
balance. The model needs to satisfy the following basic equilibrium equation: “input
= output + stock” 31,32. Here, "input" and "output" refer to the total input and total
output of P associated with a particular subsystem. "Stock change" refers to P stock in
a particular system. Since detailed import and export statistics are not available, we
assume that local products were preferred to meet local demands 30. Then, any excess
or shortfall could be treated as exports or imports, respectively 35.
We divide all the equations into three types: (1) Independent equations, in which
P flows are calculated by multiplying fixed activity data with P-intensive parameters
obtained from statistical data and field investigation. (2) Dependent equations, in
which P flows are calculated using other equations. (3) Systemic balance equations,
which are used to calculate unknown P flows or stocks by balancing all the
accounting P flows. Among the three equations, independent equations are the

=== PDF PAGE 24 ===
23
preferred types because they can reduce the interaction with other flows. Then, the
rest unknown flows are calculated by a balancing method.
(1) Independent equation: P flow is calculated by multiplying the activity data
by the coefficient：
P good goodm c   (5-1)
where P is the unknown P flow, goodm is the mass of the good, and goodc is
the concentration of P in the respective good. In some cases, however, this equation
had to be complemented to determine the mass of the product first.
(2) Dependent equations: P flow was calculated using the other equations. For
example, legacy P in cultivated land, uncultivated land, and surface water are
calculated as follows:

(3) System balance equation: calculates the unknown P flow or storage by
balancing all P flows.

5.9 P flow networks of globally traded products
The global trade P flow network encompasses PR, mineral phosphate fertilizers,
and P-containing agricultural products exchanged between various countries. The
trade flows of P-containing agricultural products occur more frequently and with
greater intensity, with 560 products categorized into 12 groups (Text S2 and Table
S2). Consequently, we analyzed the evolution of P flows embedded in agricultural
trade from 1961 to 2021.
P flow through the trade of agricultural products (P flow, Mt P) can be calculated
using the following equation (based on 2.3):

where ,i jI and ,i jE are the quantities of agricultural product i (i = 1, 2, 3,…n)
imported and exported from region j (j = 1, 2, 3,…m), respectively. iP is the P
content of agricultural product i. The P-flow quantification model followed the
approach outlined in Text S5.
A directed unweighted network (Am[t]) and a directed weighted network (Wm[t])
are based on the P product trade network from 1961 to 2021 31:

where t represents the year, m represents P ore, P fertilizer, and agricultural

degree of closeness between the trading partners, and ( ) ( )ijw t W t represent the
trade products containing P2O5 (P flow), and ( )ijw t is the P flow exported from
country (region) i to j in year t.

Text S6 Phosphorus rock resource depletion model
Frequent updates to predictions regarding the depletion of PR resources arise
from uncertainties in estimating PR reserves within Earth's crust, making the
calculation of the latest mineable years for PR critically important. Wells 27 first
proposed a depletion model for PR driven by the future demand for P. Herring and
Fantel 28 enhanced this model by simulating the depletion of known global PR
reserves and reserve bases under various population growth and future P-demand
scenarios. Van et al. 29 incorporated updated estimates of global PR reserves and their
geographic distribution using demand scenarios as exogenous inputs to simulate
phosphate depletion across various global regions. This approach accounted for the
specific depletion conditions in various countries and the cost implications of mining
PR of different grades. Building on this foundation, we extensively utilized revised
data sets of global and national PR reserve estimates, historical mining patterns (from
the USGS; refer to Text S2 and origin datasets), and projected P demand to simulate

=== PDF PAGE 48 ===
47
PR resource depletion. For future demand inputs, we referenced several scenarios of
Unconstrained Growth in Phosphate Rock Demand (UGPRD) proposed by Herring
and Fantel 28, ranging from Scenario A to Scenario J. Additionally, we incorporated
scenarios that focused on global and national population growth trends (Scenario D)
and mineral phosphate fertilizer depletion rates (Scenario C). Detailed settings for the
UGPRD scenarios and calculation methods can be found in Herring and Fantel 28 and
Van et al. 29.
Building upon these studies, we have used revised estimates of global and
national phosphate rock reserves, historical mining patterns (from the USGS, see Text
S2 and origin datasets), and future P demand to simulate phosphate rock resource
depletion. Our model improves upon previous approaches by using significantly
revised phosphate rock resource estimates for depletion definition. Compared to
earlier models, we have carefully considered the long-term historical mining
characteristics of both global and phosphate-producing countries and incorporated key
driving parameters closely related to phosphate rock demand, such as mineral
phosphate fertilizer application and population growth.
In the PRRD model, we assume that production equals demand, which becomes
increasingly effective over time as producers attempt to minimize unsold inventory 37.
For example, starting in 2017, China has strictly limited domestic phosphate rock
mining, prioritizing exports with historically mined inventories. Another
consideration in the model is boundary conditions; like typical growth models, an
initial value is required. In the case of phosphate rock, production trends fluctuate

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===
48
over time as consumption responds to production and available inventory 37. Growth
models are sensitive to the initial value; therefore, the initial production values used in
our model for the world and individual phosphate-producing countries are the average
production values from 2020 to 2023. We believe this average better reflects
production trends and eliminates some inter-annual production errors 37.
For future demand inputs, we referred to several Unconstrained Growth in
Phosphate Rock Demand (UGPRD) scenarios proposed by Herring and Fantel 37,
specifically scenarios A to J in Table S6. The phosphate rock demand growth rates are
partly based on the past 30 years of historical world production. Scenario A uses the
linear increase in global phosphate rock demand, with an annual growth rate. Linear
increases in phosphate rock demand are considered reasonable as phosphate rock
production has historically shown a high linear regression coefficient (this is also
reflected in the updated phosphate mining data in this study). At the same time, power
functions have also demonstrated excellent predictive performance (scenarios B-D),
as these functions yield relatively high regression fit coefficients for historical data
and are more appropriate than exponential growth 37. It should be noted that such
functions assume a decreasing growth rate over time, but if population growth
continues geometrically while phosphate demand follows, this assumption may be
unrealistic 37. Therefore, in addition to updating phosphate rock reserves and
production volumes, we have considered phosphate rock demand forecasts based on
historical mining patterns (Scenario B), phosphate fertilizer application characteristics
(Scenario C), and population growth trends (Scenario D). These settings align well

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===
49
with other scenarios and are useful for comparison since earlier models have often
overlooked population-driven phosphate demand growth. Scenarios E to I use varying
degrees of exponential demand growth. Scenario E uses the 1.04% annual growth rate
predicted by the WEFA study recommended by Herring and Fantel, extending it until
2005. Following that, we used a modified version of this prediction, assuming the
growth rate of 1.5% (Scenario F), which is closer to current population growth rates.
Additionally, we used growth rates of 1.7% (Scenario G), 2% (Scenario H), and 3%
(Scenario I) annually. In the final scenario (Scenario J), we established a Long
Short-Term Memory (LSTM) prediction model based on global phosphate rock
depletion trends. This model predicts short-term future demand based solely on
changes in phosphate rock mining historical trends. Overall, the comprehensive
consideration of these methods provides reliability and reference value to the
predicted outcomes of the PRRD model.
It should be noted that the static phosphate rock depletion model developed in
this study is designed to provide a preliminary assessment of the sustainability of
global and regional phosphate rock resources by combining historical extraction
patterns and future P demand, with the most up-to-date proven phosphate rock
reserves. Therefore, the model has certain limitations in projecting phosphate
depletion due to its inherent structure and assumptions, including the use of static
reserve assumptions, fixed technological and economic conditions, the absence of
behavioral and policy dynamics, and constraints on uncertainty quantification. The
model uses current proven phosphate rock reserves that are considered extractable

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
50
under existing technological and economic conditions and assumes that current
mining technologies and cost structures remain relatively stable throughout the
projection period. This represents a fundamentally “static” perspective and does not
dynamically account for potential additions to extractable reserves that may result
from increases in phosphate prices, expanded exploration efforts, or technological
advances in extraction. In addition, the model does not incorporate the impacts of
socio-economic factors such as the unpredictability of human behavior, rates of policy
adoption, regulatory delays, or the degree of compliance with sustainability measures
on P demand and resource management. Therefore, the model’s estimates should be
interpreted as a “technical potential” assessment based on specific assumptions and
constraints, reflecting the best current understanding of P resources and future
demand. It provides an essential and indispensable analytical component for assessing
the long-term challenges facing P resources.
In summary, one objective of this study is to contribute to a more systematic
assessment of the risk of P resource depletion using the above approach. Given the
multiple uncertainties involved, we do not intend to make precise predictions
regarding when P resources will be exhausted. Instead, we provide general judgments
based on estimates of available resources and future P demand. The model is
intentionally kept simple but incorporates the resource dynamics of major
phosphate-producing countries. Our aim here is to describe overall trends, such as
identifying the approximate timing when certain regions may become net importers of
P, rather than providing exact predictions for specific regions. Furthermore, we

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
51
explore the fate of P after production and consumption using a P substance flow
model and investigate effective options for reducing P use.
Text S7 Footprint from global P flow processes
7.1 Definition of human-induced P footprint
The "footprint" method is a widely used and effective tool for measuring the
environmental pressures associated with human activities 32. However, research on the
P footprint has not yet reached the level of maturity seen in other footprint indicators,
such as carbon, nitrogen, and water. Currently, there is no consensus regarding the
definition of the P-footprint concept. This study defined the human-dominated P
footprint from both resource and pollution perspectives and established an accounting
model that encompasses the entire lifecycle related to P extraction, production,
consumption, trade, waste management, and end-of-life emissions. Based on this
framework, this study analyzed the spatiotemporal distribution of the global P
footprint. For instance, the calculation of P resources and P pollution footprints for
P-related products in the consumption sector is outlined as follows 33:
_ _Footprint P Re P Pu   

7.2 Cropland P footprint of agricultural production
P is recognized as a critical resource in agricultural production because of its
essential role as a nutrient. There are two scenarios for the use of P fertilizers in food
production, distinguished by the application of artificial P fertilizers, which include
both mineral and organic fertilizers, in addition to naturally occurring soil nutrients. In
this study, we focused exclusively on the scenario involving human fertilization
because farmers in areas with permanent cultivation prioritize crop yield over nutrient
loss, even when soil P levels are in surplus 34.

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
53
The foundational data set we used included global P fertilizer application rates
and per capita fertilizer usage, which were officially recorded and applied to effective
arable land. In this context, most P losses during production occur in the early stages,
specifically in the initial P input and crop P absorption stages because crops benefit
from only a small portion of the applied P, with the remainder entering the
environment 25,35. Residual P from production is defined as the P pollution footprint
associated with crop and livestock production during the aforementioned stages.
Farmland drainage and leaching are considered to be components of the pollution
footprint.

7.3 Cropland P surplus and P footprint of leaching-runoff fractions
Most of the P entering the soil-crop system is removed during crop harvest. P
that is not absorbed by crops is either fixed in the soil, incorporated into soil organic
matter, or lost to surface water or groundwater through surface or subsurface runoff.
The loss of P from agricultural land primarily occurs through runoff, which includes
both dissolved P (soluble P) and P attached to eroded sediment particles 34.
Soil nutrient budgeting is a steady-state method that overlooks the effects of soil
organic matter accumulation on nutrient levels in positive budgets (surplus) as well as
the internal cycles of soil organic matter decomposition and mineralization. In the
absence of accumulation, a surplus indicates a potential environmental loss (for P, this
refers to runoff). A negative budget indicated P depletion in the soil 34.
To estimate the impact of agricultural non-point source P diffusion on freshwater,
we applied the "surplus method" outlined in the Gray Water Footprint Assessment
Manual 34. In this method, the P load is estimated by multiplying the P surplus by the
erosion-runoff-leaching fraction. The P surplus is determined by subtracting the P
removed through crops and crop residues from the P input via mineral and manure
fertilizers (equation 2.4-9). The erosion-runoff-leaching fraction is estimated for each
grid cell based on five influencing factors: soil texture, soil erosion vulnerability, soil
P content, rainfall intensity, and management practices. This fraction (β) varies
between the minimum (βmin) and maximum (βmax) values depending on the specific
values of these influencing factors. The cropland P surplus and P footprint of the
leaching-runoff fractions were calculated as follows:

where _ _ _Footprint Cropland P loss is the P pollution footprint from P loss from
cropland, surplusP is the P surplus from cropland, and β is the erosion-runoff-lysis
coefficient. For each influencing factor, the erosion-runoff-leaching potential (si)
score is multiplied by the weight (wi) assigned to that factor. We used the minimum
and maximum erosion-runoff-leaching ratios, along with the scores and weights for
each factor, as outlined in the Gray WaterFootprintAssessment Manual developed by an
international expert panel 34. Data on soil P content were derived from Yang et al. 36,
soil parameters, soil erosion vulnerability maps, and rainfall data were acquired from
the Google Earth Engine.
The value of β is bounded by the minimum leaching-runoff fraction (βmin) and
the maximum leaching-runoff fraction (βmax). The minimum and maximum
leaching-runoff fractions of the relevant chemical substances are listed in Table S5.
For each factor, the leaching-runoff potential score (si) is multiplied by the weight of
the factor (wi). When all influencing factors have the lowest scores (all scores are
zero), the resulting leaching-runoff fraction equals βmin. Conversely, when all factors
have the highest scores (all scores are one), the resulting leaching-runoff fraction is
equal to βmax.

=== PDF PAGE 57 ===
56
7.4 Calculation of food consumption P footprint (FCPF)
The global total food P footprint was calculated using a modified version of the
nitrogen-phosphorus calculator 7,37. This adjusted P-calculator model is an essential
tool for estimating the worldwide P footprint associated with food production and
consumption over the past 62 years 7,37,38.
Specifically, based on the modifications made by Elrys 39 to the P-calculator for
food items, we did not consider energy consumption associated with food production
and consumption when calculating the P footprint 37,38. This includes the burning of
fossil fuels for electricity generation, commodity production, transportation, and
heating. The P footprint of food (p_food) represents total P loss during food
consumption and production. P losses from food production to food consumption are
represented by the food production P footprint (FPPF). In this study, P losses during
the food-processing stage were classified as waste; therefore, our primary focus was
on food consumption, including consumption resulting from trade. It is assumed that
adults release all P from the food they consume; thus, P intake by individuals is
represented as FCPF 37. Because of the current low efficiency of technologies for
treating P-containing sludge worldwide, this study did not consider these technologies
38.
