Text S8 Future strategies for phosphorus sustainability
In sustainability scenario analysis models, given that various government
departments oversee different sustainability measures and technologies, a narrow
focus on specific outcomes within a single department may hinder P sustainability
achievements in other sectors (4). Consequently, when our model optimizes one
dimension of sustainability, it may inadvertently compromise the other dimensions.
Given this departmental fragmentation, we analyzed the impact of individual
measures on overall P sustainability and optimized the combined scenarios across
different functional departments. This approach aimed to enhance synergies across
related departments and mitigate trade-offs among the dimensions of sustainability.
Ultimately, the optimization potential of both individual and integrated measures was
quantified across the entire full-chain P flow system.
Based on an analysis of the long-term global P flow history, eight key factors
influencing P utilization and loss throughout the entire life cycle were identified:
phosphate mining, the P chemical industry, production of P-containing products,
product trade, food consumption, P recycling, waste management, and P emissions.
The objective of this study was to establish scenarios for these eight factors, with the
aim of meeting the basic P demand while maximizing the reduction in phosphate
mining and alleviating P pressure on aquatic ecosystems. This approach enables the
analysis of each country's potential to achieve a P balance within the current global
agricultural trade structure and addresses P imbalances caused by trade, thereby
enhancing our understanding of the global anthropogenic P cycle and exploring

=== PDF PAGE 60 ===
59
feasible measures for sustainable P utilization and management. Focusing on the eight
subsystems that are closely linked to the P flow process, we considered a total of 20
independent or combined management measures across six dimensions: S1 phosphate
rock resources, S2 Production, S3 Consumption, S4 Waste treatment, integrated crop–
livestock systems (ICLS), and integrated crop – livestock systems and consumption
(ICLS&C). These measures are: S1 Reduction of phosphate mining; S2.1 Reduction
of chemical fertilizer production; S2.2 Reduction of chemical fertilizer application;
S2.3 Increased feed conversion; S2.4 Integrated aquaculture; S2 Production
comprehensive measures; S3.1 Dietary shift; S3.2 Reduction of food losses; S3.3
Reduction of processing losses; S3 Consumption comprehensive measures; S4.1
Phosphogypsum and tailings recovery; S4.2 Improved straw reused as feeds; S4.3
Improved livestock manure recycle; S4.4 Improved slaughter waste utilization; S4.5
Improved human manure recycle; S4.6 Human manure not recycle; S4.7 Improved
sludge recycle; S4.8 Improved solid waste composting; S4 Waste management
comprehensive measures; and S5 Comprehensive sustainable management scenario.
The reasons and basis for choosing these scenario analysis are derived from forecasts
and visions of global P consumption demand and scenarios proposed by international
organizations or major research reports. The following text and Table S15 provide
detailed classifications, descriptions, parameter settings, and key references for these
scenario measures.
This study used the global P consumption demand projected for 2050 along with
the corresponding P flow status as the baseline scenario. It defines four predictive

=== PDF PAGE 61 ===
60
scenarios, each incorporating 2–8 specific adjustment measures, for a total of 20
scenarios. The Business-As-Usual (BAU) setting was guided by the International
Assessment of Agricultural Knowledge, Science, and Technology for Development
(Global Report) 40 and thePreferred Future PScenarios: AFrameworkforMeeting
Long-TermPNeedsforGlobalFoodDemand 41. The former offers guidance on the
consumption of all consumer products related to household use in 2050, providing
foundational data that aligns with the bottom-up P-flow model used in this study. The
latter presents predictive outcomes for phosphate mining and fertilizer use. Dietary
recommendations from the EAT-Lancet Commission provided us with a safe
operating space for the global food system 42, facilitating the evaluation of dietary and
food production practices that can contribute to achieving the United Nations
Sustainable Development Goals and the Paris Agreement. This framework establishes
the basic global food guarantee and, correspondingly, the minimum P consumption.
The 2050 population forecast data were sourced from the United Nations Population
Division's 2019 projections, using a medium-variant scenario. Based on this
information, we developed a baseline scenario and projected future P consumption
patterns for the global P flow in 2050. Given that various government departments
oversee different sustainability measures and technologies, a narrow focus on specific
outcomes within a single department may hinder P sustainability achievement in other
sectors 10. Consequently, when our model optimizes a particular sustainability
dimension, it may weaken other dimensions inadvertently. In light of this
departmental fragmentation, we analyzed the impact of individual measures on global

=== PDF PAGE 62 ===
61
P sustainability, as well as the optimization of combined scenarios from different
functional departments. This approach aimed to enhance synergies within similar
departments and mitigate trade-offs among sustainability dimensions. Ultimately, the
optimization potential of both individual and integrated measures was quantified
throughout the entire P lifecycle.
At the national level, it is theoretically possible to calculate the basic P demand
based on actual conditions such as phosphate rock resources, population size,
agricultural practices, dietary structure, and trade patterns. Therefore, the task of
exploring the potential for sustainable P use in the future is to develop scenarios
targeting these eight factors, aiming to meet basic P demand while maximizing the
reduction of phosphate mining and alleviating P pressure on aquatic ecosystems. This
enables us to analyze the potential for achieving global P balance under the current
global agricultural trade structure, address the P imbalance caused by trade, enhance
our understanding of global P cycling, and explore further feasible measures for
sustainable P use and management.
Scenario 1 (P production) involves S1.1 phosphate rock mining and S1.2
fertilizer production. We assume that the amount of phosphate rock mined and
subsequent P chemical production is used only to meet the global consumption of P
fertilizers, feed, and non-agricultural P products (BAU), halting all additional
phosphate rock mining and P chemical processing due to excessive fertilization and
food waste.

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===
62
Scenario 2 (P-containing product production) includes S2.1 reducing fertilizer
application, S2.2 improving feed conversion efficiency, and S2.3 upgrading integrated
aquaculture systems, aiming to improve P utilization efficiency in agricultural
activities. In addition to the overapplication of fertilizers, field planting experiments
conducted by Zhang et al. 42 across various regions in China indicate that differences
in crop varieties, appropriate sowing periods, tillage depth, and irrigation methods
significantly affect P utilization efficiency in farming activities (nutrient and water
use efficiency increased by 12.5%-47.0% in scientifically guided farming
experiments). We expect that by promoting innovative practices among smallholder
farmers, P utilization efficiency in cropping systems will improve to the average level
of developed countries and achieve zero fertilizer growth starting in 2030 42. In the
livestock farming sector, by improving feed formulas and breeding technologies and
incorporating more phytase to replace feed P input, P excretion can be reduced by
31.4% 43. For aquaculture, idealized scenarios are set by adjusting aquaculture feed
formulations, reducing feeding frequency, and promoting integrated aquaculture
systems. Under current technical conditions, it is feasible to increase P utilization
efficiency by 50% by 2050 44.
Scenario 3 (P-containing product processing and consumption) includes S3.1
dietary shifts, S3.2 reducing food loss, and S3.3 minimizing processing losses. Based
on the dietary recommendations proposed by the EAT–Lancet Commission (Foodin
the Anthropocene: the EAT–Lancet Commission on healthy diets from sustainable
foodsystems) 41, the Commission, comprising 19 members and 18 co-authors from 16

=== PDF PAGE 64 ===
63
countries across fields such as human health, agriculture, political science, and
environmental sustainability, established global scientific targets for healthy diets and
sustainable food production based on the best available evidence. Globally, humans
should shift from relying on animal protein to plant-based foods. This dietary shift is
flexible, considering regions where there may be insufficient animal protein intake,
and we set a target of reaching the dietary guideline recommendations by 2050. At the
same time, we adjusted the production of livestock, poultry, and fish in Scenario 2 to
meet the nutritional intake recommendations of the dietary guidelines. According to
the dietary guidelines, we estimated future demand for edible agricultural products.
Although healthy diets belong to the sustainability (SSP1) storyline—a gradual and
idealized transition to sustainable development—these dietary recommendations are
based on principles of nutritional science and considerations for preventing human
health risks. On the other hand, we referred to China’sAnti-FoodWasteWork Plan,
focusing on reducing food loss through improved technology and equipment
throughout the production and distribution process, ensuring that no food loss occurs
and that all food waste is recycled. As a flexible adjustment measure, we set a
"reduction restriction" model for Scenario 3, assuming an 80%-100% reduction in
food supply chain and processing losses (S3.3) and more than 80% of food waste
being recycled (S3.2). This reduces the pressure to increase food production and
further decreases P fertilizer use and P losses in agricultural systems.
Scenario 4 (waste management) includes S4.1 phosphogypsum soil improvement,
S4.2 improved straw feed reuse, S4.3 improved recycling of livestock manure, S4.4

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
64
improved utilization of slaughter waste, S4.5 recycling of human fecal matter, S4.6
improved recycling of sewage sludge, and S4.7 composting of solid waste from
human consumption. Utilization of tailings and preparation of fertilizers from
phosphogypsum are effective ways to utilize P tailings and phosphogypsum. For
example, the Chinese government mandates that by 2025, the comprehensive
utilization rate of new tailings and phosphogypsum should reach 60%. The
preparation of building materials can rapidly address tailings, and the use of
phosphogypsum is also being widely promoted. This study references these measures.
The OECD–FAO Agricultural Outlook 2024–2033 report and the FAO Climate
ChangeStrategy2022–2031 point out that building sustainable and resilient agri-food
systems is an effective strategy to address climate crisis challenges, helping to restore
degraded natural and artificial ecosystems 45. By 2030, zero emissions from
agricultural waste will be achieved. We assume that P loss from cropping and
livestock systems will be zero. Among them, straw reuse (S4.2, as organic fertilizer
returned to fields and as animal feed) is an effective way to promote P cycling in
farmland and re-link the planting and livestock sectors. In aquaculture, according to
the "green and healthy aquaculture" strategy proposed by the Chinese government,
continuous water purification through wastewater filtration and recovery can achieve
zero P discharge into inland freshwater and marine environments. Scenarios
S4.4-S4.7 are set based on the vision of the global Zero Waste City initiative (The
Zero Waste International Alliance, 2023; https://zwia.org/;
https://www.change.org/p/ask-united-nations-to-adopt-international-definition-of-zero

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
65
-waste). We assume zero P loss across society, with 100% recycling of slaughter
waste (S4.4), human fecal matter (S4.5), activated sludge (S4.6), and solid food waste
(S4.7).
Text S9 Supplementary explanation of future sustainable phosphorus
management approaches from a global production system
perspective
Resolving the contradiction between future food security and P sustainability is
possible, but significant nutrient and water management changes are required. Based
on the results of this study and the prospective recommendations for P sustainable use
proposed by many current studies, we present some practical and beneficial
suggestions from the perspective of the social production system to address the
increasingly imbalanced P cycle globally. These steps are crucial for finding feasible
alternatives to mineral P, closing the P resource cycle (residues and plant-available P),
and reducing the risk of environmental degradation.
9.1 Mineral resource management
For many countries, the most incredible opportunity for P management lies in
shifting from dependence on mined P to P recycling. However, for some P-importing
countries, achieving P self-sufficiency through strengthening the P circular economy
may temporarily be impossible and require significant reforms to national agricultural
systems. Therefore, ensuring fair trade in phosphate rock and mineral P fertilizers is
crucial and requires international cooperation. The World Trade Organization (WTO)
provides examples of mediation in this regard. Governments must recognize the risks

=== PDF PAGE 67 ===
66
of P supply, emphasizing the need for accurate data on reserves, resources, and supply
and demand. International schemes for raw material classification and reporting may
help standardize P data and improve accuracy. Regional agencies of the United
Nations, such as the Aarhus Convention on access to environmental information, can
help the public access data on global P reserves and fertilizer production. From a
long-term perspective, P-consuming countries, such as China, rely primarily on
domestic P ore supplies 46, so it is essential to actively promote sustainable P
management and accelerate the shift to more circular economic systems. The most
urgent task is to find effective alternatives to mineral fertilizers.
9.2 Alternatives to mineral fertilizers
The potential for replacing mineral fertilizers is evident globally, with most
countries showing significant substitution potential (Figure S16). Mineral P fertilizers
are no longer the dominant agricultural fertilizers, as P-recycling products extracted
from sewage sludge, manure, and household waste have social costs that are 81%,
50%, and 10% lower, respectively, compared to those extracted from rock-derived
superphosphate 47. Koppelaar and Weikard 48 estimated that P waste reduction and
recycling could replace over 50% (approximately 12.95 million tons) of global P
mined from rock sources annually. Advanced P recovery involves producing and
using concentrated P fertilizers from biological raw materials, replacing the
production and use of concentrated rock-based P fertilizers. This advanced P recovery
process offers opportunities to close the geochemical P cycle regionally (e.g.,
reducing long-distance transport of excess P) and systemically (e.g., at urban-rural

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===
67
interfaces), while controlling potential public health risks associated with increased
biological materials. It is expected that by 2030, P recovered from biological materials
through these advanced P recovery pathways may replace 17-31% of the P fertilizer
produced from phosphate rock in the EU, mainly due to changes in circular economy
policies, waste management, and agricultural management policies 49. Tonini et al., 47
presented the overall environmental burden of recycling P fertilizers compared to
mined P. We emphasize that this initiative (as summarized in S4) brings significant
resources (89.35% reduction in P mining) and environmental benefits (98.18%
reduction in P emissions) globally.
The countries with the highest P substitution potential are Finland (67.55% of
total P inflow), followed by Denmark (53.70%), France (47.80%), the UK (47.37%),
Austria (40.16%), Norway (38.81%), China (38.75%), and Australia (36.06%).
Sweden, Japan, and South Korea show lower indices of 24.90%, 18.91%, and 14.07%,
respectively, indicating significant proactivity. The Netherlands (4.93%) and the
United States (0.46%) show low substitution potential among exporting countries.
Agricultural production systems mainly control the high or low potential for mineral
fertilizer substitution. Whether in terms of P flow intensity, P resource demand, or P
flow processes, the agricultural sector directly influences global P resource
distribution patterns and P cycle patterns.
9.3 Potential Sources of Agricultural phosphorus
Global P resources are accelerating their depletion due to unbalanced agricultural
practices and the lack of scientific management. Reducing P losses in agricultural

=== PDF PAGE 69 ===
68
systems is crucial for enhancing global P sustainability. P accumulated in agricultural
soils and aquatic sediments, both an untapped resource and a future pollution burden,
must be addressed. Widespread soil P testing is crucial, as is proper control to prevent
over-application of P fertilizers beyond crop needs. Innovations utilizing the "legacy"
P already stored in some agricultural soils, including using P-solubilizing
microorganisms and high-efficiency P varieties, may also help 50. Solutions are not
limited to soils. In some regions, nutritional strategies in livestock production can
reduce P losses in manure. These measures include optimizing P consumption to
match animals' growth stages and adding phytase in monogastric animals to enhance
P absorption from feed 47. Collecting and returning animal manure and human excreta
to soils can reduce fertilizer demand. Encouraging changes in agricultural practices
may be more effective than merely promoting dietary changes. The importance and
contribution of the re-linking of crop production and livestock farming to efficient P
cycling are introduced in section 4.2. The third important demand-side measure is
improving agricultural efficiency, significantly reducing fertilizer demand. Promoting
optimized crop structures and efficient ecological farming practices is essential.
9.4 Farmland Management Measures
Global nutrient inputs, crop rotations, and the expansion of irrigated land could
potentially increase global major crop yields by 150% 51. "Sustainable intensification"
as a means to increase yields in underperforming landscapes while reducing the
environmental impacts of agricultural systems has also been identified as a promising
strategy, depending on specific watershed management conditions 52, to reduce land

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
69
conversion, expand natural land cover, and reduce environmental impacts 53.
Combined, these efforts can increase total food production while reducing overall
global water and nutrient usage. For example, Mueller et al. 52 estimated that by
addressing imbalances and inefficiencies could reduce the global P application on
corn, wheat, and rice by 5 million tons (38%) without affecting current yields.
Practical measures to improve the use of soil P sources (e.g., testing critical P
loss thresholds, breeding P-efficient plants, and precise agronomic management) can
reduce agricultural P inputs and P loss. For instance, China's government launched an
agricultural non-point source pollution control program in 2015, showing clear results,
with steady reductions in non-point source P and a 52.8% contribution to reducing
total P output in rivers. The national key research and development project on
fertilizer reduction and efficiency improvement during this period focused on
balanced fertilization and organic-inorganic fertilizer co-application as two key
techniques for fertilizer reduction.
Furthermore, limiting the use of organic P pesticides and promoting water-saving
irrigation techniques like drip irrigation, furrow irrigation, and shallow intermittent
irrigation (which reduces P loss by 36.4%) 54, can significantly improve P use
efficiency (PUE).
9.5 Crop Production Management
Optimizing fertilizer use across global farmland and rational allocating 16 major
crops can reduce the farmland area required to maintain current yields by nearly 50%
55. Implementing a fallow land scheme in biodiversity hotspot areas and uniformly

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
70
releasing 20% of arable land for other landscape elements could reduce almost 40%
of land demand. Barbieri et al. 51 noted that crop rotation changes will impact food
production in organic agriculture. In a scenario where all existing arable land is
converted to organic farming, the harvested area declines by 31% compared to
traditional systems. Since organic farming currently accounts for only about 1% of
global agricultural land 51, 15-20% conversion may be more feasible, such as in
Austria, where 20% of agricultural land has already been managed organically 51. A
20% organic conversion might already offer environmental and health benefits, such
as a more diversified diet 55,56. However, this is a chain process, and feeding the
global population with organic methods will require profound adjustments in human
diets and livestock production. Dietary structure changes could reduce global waste P
emissions by 27.91%, decrease new P demand by 22.15%, and reduce chemical P
inputs by 76.94% (Figures 4 in manuscript).
9.6 Food production and consumption
In the past 62 years, the average annual amount of mineral P fertilizer required
per person for food production has increased by 38%, mainly due to the consumption
and production of animal products. This has been one of the driving factors for
society advocating for grain-based diets. Thus, demand-side measures are critical. Our
results show that more rational dietary structures could significantly benefit P
resource consumption and P waste emissions. More importantly, the dietary structures
benefit human health rather than solely focusing on reducing P footprints. Similarly,
optimizing dietary structures can also minimize edible food waste during

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
71
transportation, storage, food processing, and disposal, thus reducing P losses. Overall,
changes in dietary structures could reduce global waste P emissions by 27.91%,
especially with significant environmental benefits at the levels of food production
(66.99%) and consumption (30.12%) (Figure 4 in manuscript). At the same time,
public awareness of the environmental impact of consuming high-P products needs to
be raised to support more sustainable food choices. With the support of food labeling
and public education, consumers can play a role in reducing human P demand by
avoiding overconsumption of high-P footprint foods and reducing food waste.
However, the situation is particularly complex for imported products, as they contain
ingredients from multiple countries, which may cause eutrophication impacts in their
countries of origin. Governments urgently need to support more P-sustainable food
systems by setting organic waste recycling targets, reducing meat production
subsidies, and taxing food waste disposal through landfills and/or incineration.
9.7 Waste Management Sector
The transition to a circular P economy (i.e., where waste is no longer considered
waste) should have already occurred. Although there are many methods to recover P
from wastewater and other organic materials, investing in driving market forces to
increase the use of recycled P in fertilizers is necessary. economic, legislative, and
promotional measures are required to help the mineral fertilizer industry use recycled
P as a raw material to increase P recycling significantly. The main improvement on
the supply side lies in recycling measures, as the P peak may occur around 2035, and
the static reserve lifetime of P is 120 years (Figure S5, US Geological Survey, 2007).

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===
72
For example, phosphate gypsum improvement (S4.1) can increase the P recovery of
chemical systems by 71.43% and reduce P loss in mining systems by 60%, equivalent
to extending the global mining lifespan by 30 years. In addition to fiscal incentives, a
management framework can also help promote using regenerated P fertilizers in the
existing fertilizer market. Switzerland and Germany are leading the way in this regard.
Currently, Switzerland shows a relatively closed P cycle, with P reused from waste
(wastewater) systems accounting for 88% of the total P input to farmland. France
(55%) and Sweden (69%) also show satisfactory results, while countries like the
Netherlands (2%), the United Kingdom (11%), and Austria (15%) have low recycling
rates. Assuming a 100% recovery rate of P from waste systems, Switzerland could
fully replace mineral fertilizers (117%). In comparison, the Netherlands and the
United Kingdom could reduce their use of minerals by 91% and 74%, respectively. If
all surface water and landfill P losses were avoided nationally, they could replace
50% (France) and 193% (Switzerland) of annual mineral fertilizer input. The most
important secondary P resource remains manure, which has the highest P plant
availability and a large enough quantity (10.9 thousand tons of plant-available P/year)
to meet the entire P fertilizer demand in some countries (e.g., Norway, 5.8 thousand
tons of plant-available P/year).
9.8 Urban Sector
Urbanization, although bringing dense populations and highly concentrated P
hotspot areas, is good news for collecting and recycling urban waste P. Urban sewage
treatment facilities can provide significant amounts of secondary P, especially as

=== PDF PAGE 74 ===
73
numerous scientists worldwide are relentlessly researching activated sludge P
recovery technologies. Reusing sewage sludge can save 1.16 MT of P resources,
improve P recovery rates in waste systems by 75.55%, and reduce the P pollution
footprint by 6.25% across the entire system. Similarly, processed waste (3.13 MT P),
food waste (13.81 MT P), and human excreta (4.54 MT P) generated during human
consumption contain staggering amounts of P, especially when P is collected from
densely populated areas. Without considering other P management measures,
returning human feces to fields can reduce 3.26% of global new P input, 11.33% of P
fertilizer input, 33.88% of P emissions from waste systems, and increase P recovery
by 188.33% in the consumption system. Similarly, food waste shows remarkable P
recovery potential, reducing 16.37% of P mining and increasing P recovery by
153.04% and 286.17% in the consumption and waste systems, respectively. This
strongly highlights the severe issue of global-scale P waste in consumption systems
and the urgent need for P recovery (potential). These P resources undoubtedly find
their best destination in intensive farmlands, where they can directly reduce excessive
dependence on mineral P. These measures for the urban sector are estimated to extend
the global P mine life by 40 years.
9.9 Aquatic Resource Management Actions
There is a need to reduce P loss from the source to the ocean, ensuring broad
resonance, especially when dealing with transboundary waters and large marine
ecosystems. The building blocks of this integrated approach are already in place. For
example, the United Nations Environment Program’s freshwater ecosystem

=== PDF PAGE 75 ===
74
management framework guides countries to sustainably manage freshwater
ecosystems, including setting P targets. Several existing international institutions,
including the UN Water Convention, the Law of the Sea Convention, and the
Convention on Biological Diversity, can strengthen regional actions against P
pollution 16. However, for some water bodies with historically severe P pollution,
merely reducing contemporary P input may not be enough to restore ecological and
socioeconomic conditions. New measures are being developed to address this issue.
For example, although controversial, geoengineering measures have been proposed to
address the symptoms of nutrient pollution in the Baltic Sea.
It is essential to deeply understand the social P flow processes at the watershed
level and actively build a P cycle within watersheds. Efforts should focus on
improving P use efficiency, waste management, and how P enters freshwater systems,
particularly in agricultural production and daily life. PUE and P Storage Efficiency
(PSE) are key indicators in the P transport process and are more directly observed and
regulated in watershed management. P buffering capacity (PBF), as a comprehensive
measure of a watershed's ability to retain residual P, is considered a key regulatory
service that can limit the release of legacy P and reduce agricultural production and
expansion’s impact on water quality 57,58. This aligns with the “sustainable
intensification” management reform proposed by Mueller et al., 52 which addresses
the pressure of achieving truly sustainable P use in farmland. For rural wastewater and
agricultural nonpoint source wastewater, considering the direction of P loading into
freshwater systems, pollution interception, and control projects should be built around

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
75
lakes/rivers and runoff pollution control projects in intensive farmlands to control
agricultural pollution.
9.10 International intergovernmental efforts
Currently, many regions lack sustainable P management strategies.
Intergovernmental bodies have taken little action regarding transboundary P pollution
or the transport of phosphate rock pollutants in mineral P fertilizers. Similarly, there
are very few national policies on sustainable P management and none on a global
scale. Current intergovernmental frameworks are fragmented, which may result in
collective knowledge on P sustainability remaining isolated, with little
communication between them. These institutions must work systematically to ensure
socioeconomic and environmental benefits globally.
Sustainable P resource management requires global cooperation, especially in the
areas of technology transfer and knowledge sharing. Developed countries should
provide technical assistance to developing countries, especially P mining and reserve
countries, to help them achieve P resource recovery and reuse. International
organizations such as UNEP and the World Bank should strengthen global
cooperation on P resource management, promoting sharing best practices and
technologies across countries. In addition, developed countries should take greater
environmental responsibility in global P trade, paying environmental costs to P
mining countries to support more sustainable management measures during resource
extraction. Developed countries that rely on P imports should pay environmental costs
to Morocco and other P mining countries through the Polluter Pays Principle (PPP).

=== PDF PAGE 77 ===
76
Furthermore, global P resource management policies should be promoted,
particularly regarding P mining, trade, and recycling. Data on P mining, production,
trade, and consumption should be collected and managed in a unified manner to
establish a global P resource flow database. By sharing data, countries can enhance
their global perspective on P resource use and promote policy coordination and
rational P resource allocation, avoiding over-dependence on P mining and
consumption in a single country or region. The database can also be a foundation for
international P management cooperation, providing reliable data support for
formulating global P mining and trade policies. The database can summarize the
impacts of P mining on soil, water resources, and ecosystems and provide relevant
sustainability indicators. For example, based on indicators such as tailings treatment,
wastewater discharge, and energy consumption, the environmental burden of P
mining in different countries and regions can be assessed, providing a basis for
promoting green mining and environmental technology applications. According to the
Polluter Pays Principle (PPP), both P mining and consuming countries should share
the environmental costs of P resource extraction and use. For example, P mining
countries like Morocco could levy environmental or green taxes on exported
phosphate rock to ensure that the environmental impacts of their mining processes are
compensated.
The "International Call for Action on P Issues" seeks to establish or expand an
intergovernmental coordination mechanism similar to the one developed for nitrogen
issues. This mechanism should help governments, existing conventions,

=== PDF PAGE 78 ===
77
intergovernmental frameworks, and stakeholders promote comprehensive action on P
sustainability. An international framework must be applied to consolidate collective
knowledge on national-to-global P cycles, set internationally agreed targets for P
management improvements, and quantify the economic and social benefits of
improving P sustainability. Future resolutions on P at the United Nations
Environment Assembly will be a key opportunity to mobilize intergovernmental
action to achieve these goals and represent a strong commitment to supporting
transformation 16.
Text S10 Limitations and uncertainty analysis
The comprehensive process analysis framework we established for global P flow
and metabolism from a full lifecycle perspective enables dynamic assessment of the
global anthropogenic P cycle. Our integrated model has some limitations that can be
improved and explored. Overall, these limitations mainly relate to the
comprehensiveness of activity data in quantifying P flow (including considerations of
trade levels), the reliability of empirical coefficients, the comprehensiveness of SFA
model construction, uncertainties in scenario settings in the scenario analysis, and
uncertainties in the calculation process. These limitations present an opportunity to
develop a more advanced integrated assessment model to understand global P
behavior better and formulate sustainable development strategies. Therefore, we
provide a detailed explanation of limitations or uncertainty analysis and aim to offer
solutions to ensure the research plan's scientific integrity and the reliability of the
results. We also provide specific information for future model improvements.

=== PDF PAGE 79 ===
78
Specifically, the uncertainties in this study primarily arise from the underlying
data and the model construction for P flow accounting. The SFA model is highly
dependent on empirical coefficients, making uncertainty of these coefficients one of
the most critical sources of overall uncertainty. Available coefficients for global-scale
P flow studies are limited, and P content varies across different products, literature
sources, and regions. To address this, we quantified these uncertainties using Monte
Carlo simulations, providing probability distributions for key P flows and establishing
confidence intervals for major results (see Figure S20), thereby enhancing the
robustness of our findings.
Additionally, incomplete data coverage introduces uncertainty in model
construction. For instance, our trade analysis considers only international trade,
omitting subnational or regional trade, which may slightly overestimate P flows (e.g.,
intra-European trade). Future research could incorporate local and subnational trade
data to explore P flows at finer spatial scales. Uncertainty in scenario analysis, though
often overlooked, is equally critical. Our scenario analysis evaluates the technical
potential of interventions, representing the maximum achievable improvements under
ideal conditions. In practice, the realized potential may be constrained by factors such
as the speed and effectiveness of policy implementation, variability in human
behavior and technology adoption, and regional disparities in economic development.
Therefore, while scenario results provide valuable benchmarks for policy and
strategic planning, the actual improvements may fall short of the predicted technical
potential.

=== PDF PAGE 80 ===
79
Despite these limitations, the macro-scale patterns and major P flow trends
remain robust. By explicitly identifying key sources of uncertainty and providing
mitigation strategies, we reduce their impact and offer guidance for future studies.
The Monte Carlo simulations indicate that estimations of key P flows fluctuate within
reasonable ranges, supporting their use in policy-making and sustainability
assessments. Future improvements should focus on enhancing data completeness,
updating empirical coefficients, integrating regional trade and environmental
interactions, and accounting for uncertainties in socio-economic behavior to further
improve the predictive accuracy and applicability of the model. The detailed sources
of uncertainty and corresponding analyses are presented in the following sections.
10.1 Uncertainty of underlying data
We utilized data on agricultural product production, imports and exports, food
supply, and food waste collected from the FAO database. According to studies on
anthropogenic P cycling (Table S1), although the FAOSTAT database is not complete,
it provides the most comprehensive agricultural product (exchange) data currently
available for depicting global P flow scenarios 5. Import and export data for
P-containing products from the World Trade Organization (WTO) serve as
supplementary or calibration data for trade information. Phosphate rock data were
obtained from the United States Geological Survey (USGS). We would like to
emphasize that while the social activity data comes from a single source, the official
database provides authoritative foundational data, ensuring a relatively low level of
uncertainty. However, for SFA models that heavily rely on activity data, future

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===
80
research should focus on enhancing the comprehensiveness and accuracy of the
foundational data.
10.2 Uncertainty in analytical models of global trade
In this study's global trade analysis model, we focused solely on international
trade flows and did not consider the impact of subnational/regional trade on the global
P cycle. While research on local agricultural product trade is limited, studies suggest
that the specialization of local agricultural systems has driven significant
transportation of feed and food, affecting national nutrient cycles and recycling
barriers 24. However, it is rare to describe the characteristics of local trade in a
consistent, comprehensive, and homogeneous database, making it nearly impossible
to assess this information on a global scale. We also note that, given the international
trade among European countries, we may have overestimated the impact of trade.
These countries, though geographically small, are part of the same supranational
union. For instance, eliminating intra-regional exchanges between Western and
Eastern Europe would reduce P flows via agricultural trade by approximately 0.47
MT (i.e., 19%). Similar regional exchanges may occur between states or provinces in
large countries like the United States, India, or China. Still, we did not account for
this in our study due to a lack of standardized data. On the other hand, net P imports
through agricultural trade may influence P accumulation in countries. While
considering potential P re-export through processed products is important, we did not

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
81
account for this in our analysis. These results change only slightly when expressed in
terms of cropland and permanent crop area.
10.3 Uncertainty in the quantitative modelling and effective solutions
We note that uncertainties are associated with the study in aspects of the flow
quantification model and datasets. To minimize these uncertainties, we conducted
cross-checks between alternative calculation methods for each P flow to identify the
most suitable approach. For instance, instead of multiplying the excreta generation
rate by the average P concentration, we calculated P in human excreta by multiplying
the actual P intake by the observed human metabolism. This is because the former
method could yield a P value in human excreta that exceeds actual P intake, which
would be contradictory. Some subsystems' imbalances between inputs and outputs are
used as a cross-check (Text S5 details the SFA model calculation process and the
accounting model used for cross-validation).
In this study, we only analyzed P fluxes in socio-economic systems and did not
consider the relationship with the natural environment. Therefore, this study may not
present clear criteria for the environmental impact of P-related metabolic activities.
However, we used the SFA model and the Cropland P surplus and P footprint of
leaching-runoff fractions model to calculate the flux of P from the societal system to
the surface water system, respectively.
Standardizing all data collection activities is challenging, which may introduce
uncertainties in our analysis. For instance, although we used median values from
multiple sources, variations in P content across different products and potential

=== PDF PAGE 83 ===
82
statistical inaccuracies could affect the results. Furthermore, a relatively high level of
uncertainty is associated with runoff and erosion from agricultural soils, as we
assumed a linear relationship between surface P balance and runoff/erosion. However,
actual erosion varies significantly depending on soil type, slope, and tillage practices.
Similarly, while most P entering surface water can be calculated using separate
equations, specific P flows related to the water environment remain difficult to
determine accurately. For example, P losses to water during food processing are
primarily calculated using mass balance equations. Furthermore, different data
sources may introduce uncertainties in our results. For example, the P content (P) in
various materials, such as crops, excreta, and animal products, is estimated from
relevant literature. While we made some adjustments before the calculations, some
inaccuracies may still exist. Lastly, our study focuses solely on P flows and does not
consider other types of flows, such as energy flows or financial flows, which could
potentially influence the results.
10.4 Uncertainty in the empirical coefficients and effective solutions
The SFA model heavily relies on the accuracy of empirical coefficients. Given
that global studies involve a wide range of regions and countries, the updating and
calibrating of empirical coefficients are crucial for future model improvements,
especially in the process of P entering the natural environmental system from the
socio-ecological system. These limitations present opportunities for further research,
including a deeper understanding of P behavior and developing a more advanced
integrated model encompassing a broader range of environmental media and flow

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===
83
pathways. These improvements will provide a more comprehensive global assessment
of the human-induced P cycle and strategies for P sustainability.
The data used in the bottom-up SFA approach are sourced from multiple origins,
exhibiting varying degrees of data uncertainty. Therefore, we conducted a Monte
Carlo (MC) simulation to assess the uncertainties of the parameters quantitatively
35,59,60. This was accomplished by providing a range for all data instead of relying on
single-point estimates. It should be noted that it was impossible to obtain sufficient
empirical coefficients due to the limitations of global-scale P flow studies and the
large number of P-containing products involved in the calculations. In this study, we
tried to collect empirical coefficients for each subsystem based on previous studies to
support the Monte Carlo simulations. The SFA model used for cross-validation can
independently perform MC simulations. Text S5 and Table S4 provide the
computational process, parameter ranges, and data sources used for cross-validation.
As only a single source was available for activity data (governmental statistical
yearbooks and bulletins), the MC simulation did not incorporate uncertainties
associated with activity data. Considering the heterogeneous sources of the
parameters, continuous distributions (triangular, uniform, or normal) were employed
to account for the likelihood of their occurrence while considering data quality (see
Table S4 for details). Overall, we assumed independence between input data and
independently sampled them from their respective distributions. Once the
probabilities of the selected input data were determined, the MC simulation was used
to calculate the probability distributions of the model's output results. This model

=== PDF PAGE 85 ===
84
computed the uncertainty associated with the P input-output for each subsystem in the
socio-economic system, as well as the major P flows. To achieve this, the dataset was
randomly sampled based on the probability distributions presented in Table S4. The
output of this model runs with Python, and the number of runs is set to 10000. The
uncertainty analysis results (interval distribution) of key P flows are presented in
Figure S20.
10.5 Uncertainty of scenario analysis
This study employs scenario analysis to quantify the “technical potential ” of
various intervention measures in improving global P sustainability, including resource
conservation, enhanced PUE, and reduced emissions. The scenarios represent the
conditions that could be achieved when governmental policies and human activities
fully meet their intended targets, with an emphasis on assessing the maximum
benefits that are technically and operationally feasible. Therefore, the model outputs
primarily reflect potential improvements under ideal or fully implemented conditions,
that is, the technical potential. However, we fully recognize that the real-world
implementation of these strategies is subject to substantial uncertainties and is
strongly influenced by socioeconomic factors. These factors include, but are not
limited to, the unpredictability of human behavior, actual policy adoption rates,
compliance with sustainability measures, technology deployment costs, political
willingness, and disparities in regional economic development. In assessing technical
potential, the model does not explicitly incorporate these complex socioeconomic
feedback and uncertainties as quantifiable parameters. As a result, the estimated

=== PDF PAGE 86 ===
85
technical potential may exceed the practical implementation potential, as the full
on-ground realization of strategies is often constrained or delayed by these
human-driven factors.
This study primarily focuses on evaluating technical potential, and the scenario
analysis results represent the achievable potential under the stated assumptions.
Although it has limitations in capturing complex socioeconomic dynamics, we
emphasize that it provides an essential benchmark for informing policy design and
strategic planning. Future work will aim to develop more advanced modeling
frameworks that better integrate dimensions such as human behavioral shifts, policy
delays, adoption rates, and other socioeconomic drivers, thereby more accurately
reflecting the uncertainties and challenges associated with the real-world
implementation of sustainability scenarios.
10.6 Sensitivity analyses
10.6.1 One-factor-at-a-time sensitivity analysis method
Sensitivity analysis is a method used to evaluate the extent to which small
changes in key variables within a model affect the outcomes. By quantifying the
relationship between fluctuations in variables and changes in results, it helps identify
the factors to which the outcomes are most sensitive, providing a basis for risk focus
and robustness assessment and thereby validating the model's reliability. The core
principle of sensitivity analysis is the one-at-a-time (OAT) approach, where the value
of a single variable is altered while keeping all other variables constant, either
individually or in combination, and the resulting trend in outcomes is observed. By

=== PDF PAGE 87 ===
86
comparing the differences in outcome fluctuations caused by changes in different
variables, the "sensitivity" of each variable can be determined; greater fluctuations
indicate higher sensitivity.
In this study, a univariate sensitivity analysis was conducted for both the
production and trade stages. Based on historical data, reasonable ranges of variation
for each variable were defined. Other variables were fixed while the target variable
was altered, and the resulting changes in the outcome indicators were repeatedly
calculated. The specific calculation formula is given in Equation (10.1). By
calculating sensitivity coefficients, the relative impact of different variables on the
outcome indicators can be clearly compared, providing quantitative support for the
robustness of the model.
Sensitivity coefficient (SC)=
Rate of change in outcome indicators ΔR/R0
Rate of change in variables ΔV/V0
（10.1）
R0 denotes the initial value of the outcome indicator;ΔR denotes the change in
the outcome indicator (ΔR=R1−R0); V0 denotes the initial value of the variable; ΔV
denotes the change in the variable (ΔV=V1−V0).
The results of the sensitivity analysis in this study were primarily visualized
using boxplots and tables. Boxplots, as an efficient statistical visualization tool,
effectively illustrate the distribution differences, dispersion, and outliers among
multiple data sets in this study. This visualization approach not only efficiently
condenses key information from multidimensional data, facilitating rapid comparison
of sensitivity characteristics across different products, but also intuitively highlights
data anomalies, providing clear guidance for subsequent checks on model accuracy

=== PDF PAGE 88 ===
87
and refinement of analytical logic. Tabular presentation of the results ranks the
sensitivity coefficients of 279 products (568 products for the sensitivity analysis of
trade), offering readers a complete and clear view of the sensitivity analysis results
and detailed characteristics.

variables are entered at a time), which is divided into two types: raw-scale regression
and standardized regression. The former is used for model fitting and evaluation,
while the latter is used for sensitivity analysis.

as “total” in the code), the assumed linear relationship is:

Measures the average prediction error. Its unit is the same as the target variable,

It is essentially “the square root of the mean squared residuals” and reflects the
absolute magnitude of prediction errors.

10.6.3 Sensitivity analysis of global P flows
This study systematically evaluated the sensitivity of global P flows to product
production, trade, and interaction effects using sensitivity coefficients and multiple
linear regression, providing a comprehensive identification of key uncertainties and

=== PDF PAGE 97 ===
96
their contributions to total P flow. Sensitivity coefficients reflect the extent to which
changes in product output affect the total P flow. A higher coefficient indicates
greater sensitivity of the total flow to that product, meaning that fluctuations in its
production have a more significant impact. Following a product classification scheme,
we categorized data for 280 products globally from 1961 to 2022 into 12 categories:
Fruits and Vegetables, Animal Products, Cereals, Non-food Agricultural Products,
Alcoholic and Beverages, Cakes, Live Animals, Forage, Pulses, Aquatic Products,
Kitchen Condiments, and Raw Materials. Based on numerical ranges, the twelve
product categories were further divided into high, medium, and low sensitivity levels.
A detailed description of the multifactor sensitivity analysis is provided in Section
10.5.2.
The results indicate that global P flows are highly dependent on a few core
product categories, while most other products exert limited influence. Overall, global
P flow uncertainties are primarily driven by fluctuations in Cereals and Animal
Products production and trade. Minor products individually contribute little, but
certain combinations can amplify their effects through interactions along the industry
chain. The interaction analysis further emphasizes complex interdependencies within
the production and trade system. These findings highlight that precise management
and forecasting of core products and their trade flows are essential for mitigating P
flow volatility, controlling environmental risks, and optimizing resource allocation
globally.

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===
97
Specifically, in the production of global products, Cereals exhibited the highest
sensitivity, with coefficients ranging from 0.43 to 0.56, peaking at 0.56 in 1976. The
coefficient declined gradually from 0.52–0.54 in 1961 to 0.43–0.45 in 2020 but
remained substantially higher than other categories, maintaining its dominant role in
total P flow. Animal Products had sensitivity coefficients of 0.23–0.29, increasing
from 0.25 in 1961 to 0.29 in 2020, indicating an increasingly strong driving effect.
Medium-sensitivity categories included Fruits and Vegetables, Raw Materials, and
Cakes, collectively contributing approximately 15%–20% of total P flow.
Low-sensitivity categories, including Non-food Agricultural Products, Kitchen
Condiments, Alcoholic and Beverages, and Forage, exerted minimal influence and
displayed limited fluctuations.
Within Cereals, wheat, rice, and maize consistently accounted for 70%–85% of
total production, directly determining the stability and direction of total P flow. Minor
crops such as black wheat and quinoa had negligible influence individually but
indicated increasing diversification in cereal composition. In Non-food Agricultural
Products, cotton lint exhibited particularly high sensitivity (0.83–0.89), dominating
category-level P flow fluctuations. In Fruits and Vegetables, dry onions and shallots
were the core products, with sensitivity coefficients of 0.11–0.20 (mean 0.15), while
other products showed low variability.
For global P trade, exports of Cereals had the highest impact, with an average
sensitivity coefficient of 0.47, and substantial intra-category dispersion. Wheat
exports exerted the strongest influence, with a mean coefficient of 0.22 and relatively

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
98
high variability. Soybeans were less influential (mean 0.11) but still contributed
significantly to trade-related P flows. Pulses and Cakes showed medium sensitivity,
while low-impact categories such as Live Animals were primarily influenced by cattle,
whose sensitivity declined from 0.70 in 1961 to 0.36 in 2022. Overall, trade-related
sensitivity patterns mirrored production-side characteristics, with core categories
dominating global P trade flows.
Standardized multiple linear regression identified core sensitive variables
including Animal Products, Cereals, Raw Materials, Fruits and Vegetables, and Pulses.
Animal Products showed the highest mean sensitivity coefficient in multivariable
combinations, reaching 1.2914, indicating a strong driving effect on total P flow.
Interaction-term analysis revealed both synergistic and antagonistic effects: Cereals
exhibited positive synergistic effects in 23 combinations, amplifying total P output,
while Pulses were predominantly antagonistic, suppressing P flow fluctuations in
43.5% of combinations. Live Animals combined with Forage displayed synergistic
effects (sensitivity coefficient 0.121, R²=0.977), highlighting amplification within the
production chain. Model training and test R² exceeded 0.98, with controllable
prediction errors, confirming the robustness and reliability of the results.
10.6.3.1 Sensitivity analysis of global P flows from the production of different
products
In the production of global products, Cereals (0.43–0.56) and Animal Products
(0.23–0.29) were classified as high-sensitivity categories, dominating the total P flow
and collectively accounting for more than 70%. Fruits and Vegetables (0.05–0.07),

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
99
Raw Materials (0.03–0.06), and Cakes (0.02–0.06) were assigned to the
medium-sensitivity level, together representing approximately 15%–20%. Non-food
Agricultural Products (0.0009–0.016), Kitchen Condiments (0.0002–0.0005), Other
(0.0007–0.0014), Alcoholic and Beverages (0.0001–0.0048), and Forage
(0.0005–0.002) were classified as low sensitivity, exerting minimal influence.
Cereals are the most sensitive category, with sensitivity coefficients consistently
above 0.43 from 1961 to 2022, peaking in 1976 at 0.5597 and showing a gradual
declining trend from 0.52–0.54 in 1961 to 0.43–0.45 in 2020. Despite the decrease,
the coefficients remain substantially higher than those of other categories, maintaining
Cereals as the core determinant of total P flow. Animal Products rank second in
sensitivity, showing an increasing trend from 0.2546 in 1961 to 0.2935 in 2020.
Within the medium-sensitivity category, Fruits and Vegetables exhibited relatively
stable coefficients with low variability, with a standard deviation of only 0.0040. Raw
Materials showed a steady increase in sensitivity, rising from 0.0333 in 1961 to
0.0563 in 2022, representing an increase of approximately 69.1%. The sensitivity
coefficients of Cakes showed an overall decreasing trend, from 0.0528 in 1961 to
0.0220 in 2022. Low-sensitivity product categories exerted minimal influence on total
P flows and displayed limited fluctuation.
Among the seventeen crops in the Cereals category (Figures S21-S22), wheat,
rice, and maize consistently accounted for 70%–85% of the total, with their
production fluctuations directly determining the stability and direction of the total P
flow structure. Crops contributing less than 1%, such as broad beans, buckwheat,

=== PDF PAGE 101 ===
100
quinoa, and black wheat, had minor impacts, but the emergence of previously absent
minor crops reflects diversification in the structure. Although minor crops such as
black wheat and quinoa have minimal influence, they signify a structural shift in
Cereals from being dominated by traditional staple grains to a more diversified crop
composition. This indicates that from 1961 to 2022, different cereal crops had
markedly different impacts on P flows, with wheat contributing most significantly
within the Cereals category.
Within the Non-food Agricultural Products category, cotton lint (ginned)
displayed particularly high sensitivity, with coefficients ranging from 0.83 to 0.89
from 1961 to 2021 and an average of approximately 0.85, far exceeding other
products. This indicates that cotton lint dominates the total of Non-food Agricultural
Products, with its production changes contributing most to the category's total P flow
fluctuations, making it the core driver of P variability in this group. In the Fruits and
Vegetables category, the core product was onions and shallots, dry (excluding
dehydrated), with sensitivity coefficients ranging from 0.11 to 0.20 and an average of
0.15, higher than other products in the category. Except for sweet potatoes, which
exhibited relatively high variability (standard deviation 0.04), other products showed
minor impacts with low variability.
Sensitivity analysis results for the remaining products are provided in Figures
S21-S23, Table S11, and Supplementary Dataset.

=== PDF PAGE 102 ===
101
10.6.3.2 Sensitivity analysis of global P flows from the trade of different products
A sensitivity analysis was conducted for 568 export products to examine the
influence of their export volumes on global P trade flows. These 568 products were
categorized into 13 groups for analysis (see Section 10.5.2). Overall, exports of
products in the Cereals category had a significant impact on global P trade flows, with
the highest sensitivity observed for this category. The mean sensitivity coefficient for
Cereals was 0.47, and the coefficients within this category exhibited substantial
dispersion, indicating a wide range of impact fluctuations. Among these, wheat
exports had the largest effect on P flows, with a mean coefficient of 0.22 and
relatively large variability compared to other products. Soya beans, with a mean of
0.11, exerted a weaker influence than wheat but still played a significant role in P
flow, noticeably higher than other products. Pulses and Cakes had comparable
impacts on P flows, but the sensitivity coefficients for Pulses mostly fell below the
mean, whereas those for Cakes were concentrated above the mean. This indicates that,
for most years, exports of Cakes products had a greater influence on P flows than
Pulses. The remaining product categories had limited impact and relatively small
fluctuations. These sensitivity analysis results are consistent with the patterns of
global P interactions induced by agricultural trade presented in the main manuscript.
The 12 product categories were further classified into three impact levels: high,
medium, and low. Cereals were classified as high impact; Pulses and Cakes as
medium impact; and all remaining categories as low impact. Due to analytical
complexity, representative cases from each impact level were selected for focused

=== PDF PAGE 103 ===
102
analysis. Within Cereals, wheat was the core product with a mean sensitivity
coefficient of 0.45, followed by maize (corn), while other products had minimal
influence and small coefficient fluctuations. For Pulses, soya beans exhibited
relatively high values, reflecting improvements in soybean cultivation technology and
strong market demand, with sensitivity coefficients rising from approximately 0.691
in 1961 to nearly 0.9 in 2022. Other products in this category maintained very low
and stable values in most years, indicating a minor contribution to overall
trade-related P flows.
In the low-impact category, live animals were primarily influenced by cattle,
which showed substantial variability. Due to market demand, the sensitivity
coefficient for cattle declined from 0.70 in 1961 to 0.36 in 2022. Most other products
within this category had limited influence on total P flows of live animals, with small
fluctuations.
Detailed results of the sensitivity analysis for trade-related P flows of individual
products are provided in Figure S23, Table S12, and Supplementary Dataset.
10.6.3.3 Sensitivity analysis of global P flow using standardized multiple linear
regression and interaction effects
In multiple linear regression, the core meaning of the sensitivity coefficient
(standardized regression coefficient) is that “when an independent variable changes
by one standard deviation, the dependent variable changes on average by a certain
number of standard deviations.” The absolute value of the coefficient directly reflects
the strength of a variable’s influence on the dependent variable. By statistically

=== PDF PAGE 104 ===
103
analyzing the sensitivity coefficients (absolute values) of all possible combinations of
variables, the global impact of each variable can be clarified. In this study, the dataset
was divided into training and test sets, and only results with P-values <0.05 were
considered valid to ensure statistical reliability.
In the bivariate linear regression analysis (standardized regression without
interaction terms), variables were categorized into two groups: core sensitive
variables (mean absolute sensitivity coefficient ≥0.6) and weakly sensitive variables
(mean absolute sensitivity coefficient <0.6). In global P-containing product
production, core sensitive variables included “Cereals,” “Animal products,” “Raw
materials,” “Fruits and vegetables,” and “Pulses.” Among them, “Animal products”
exhibited the highest mean sensitivity coefficient, particularly in the combination of
“Kitchen condiments” and “Animal products,” where the coefficient reached 1.2914.
This indicates that fluctuations in “Animal products” have the strongest driving effect
on total output and can significantly propagate through the entire industry chain.
Weakly sensitive variables included “Kitchen condiments,” “Cakes,” “Non-food
agricultural products,” “Alcohol and beverages,” and “Forage.” Most of these
variables displayed insufficient sensitivity or statistical significance (mean <0.6, some
combinations with P≥0.05). Still, when combined with “Fruits and vegetables” (e.g.,
“Fruits and vegetables” + “Kitchen condiments”), their sensitivity could reach the
level of core variables due to consumption scenario linkage and partial industry chain
overlap (coefficient 1.2914).

=== PDF PAGE 105 ===
104
In the trade P-SFA, the test set R² of all variable combinations exceeded 0.93.
Among the top 10 combinations ranked by test set R² (e.g., “Cereals” + “Animal
products,” “Fruits and vegetables” + “Cereals,” “Animal products” + “Cakes”), test
set R² exceeded 0.996. Differences between training and test set R² were minimal, and
prediction errors were controllable, indicating that the model can effectively predict
and analyze trade volumes. Core sensitive variables included “Animal products,”
“Fruits and vegetables,” “Cakes,” “Cereals,” “Pulses,” and “Alcohol and beverages,”
with combinations involving “Animal products” achieving test set R² ≥0.9974 and a
mean sensitivity coefficient of 1.08, highlighting its critical role in improving model
fit. Other variables generally exhibited lower sensitivity coefficients or were not
statistically significant..
Considering that the basic multiple linear regression only quantifies the
independent effect of a single variable and cannot capture synergistic or antagonistic
interactions, this study further introduced interaction-term analysis. For production
variables, high-quality combinations were selected in which cross-sensitivity
coefficients, interaction term P-values, and all individual variable coefficients were
significant, totaling 36 combinations. Training set R² exceeded 0.645, and test set R²
exceeded 0.746. Among these, 23 combinations exhibited antagonistic effects, 5
showed synergistic enhancement, and 8 showed no significant interaction. In
antagonistic combinations, 43.5% involved “Pulses,” with cross-sensitivity
coefficients significantly higher than other antagonistic combinations (≥0.056),
indicating a strong suppressive effect on total output. In synergistic combinations, 4

=== PDF PAGE 106 ===
105
out of 5 involved “Cereals,” and all interactions with other variables were positive,
demonstrating that “Cereals” can significantly amplify the positive effect on total
output.
In trade P-SFA, 55 complete variable combinations were examined, with test set
R² ranging from 0.953 to 0.999 and a mean of 0.991; 96.4% of combinations had test
set R² ≥0.98, indicating excellent explanatory power and high predictive accuracy for
total P flow. Interaction analysis revealed 22 synergistic enhancement combinations,
17 antagonistic combinations, and 16 non-significant interaction, with a substantial
increase in the proportion of synergistic effects. Among 11 combinations involving
“Live animals,” 72.7% were synergistic, with a mean synergistic coefficient of 0.086,
higher than the overall mean (0.062). For example, “Live animals” + “Forage” had a
synergistic coefficient of 0.121 (R²=0.977), showing a significant positive
amplification on total P flow. “Pulses” remained primarily antagonistic, with all
related combinations exhibiting cross-sensitivity coefficients ≥0.098, such as “Pulses”
+ “Cakes” (R²=0.994, antagonistic coefficient -0.142) and “Kitchen condiments” +
“Pulses” (R²=0.991, antagonistic coefficient -0.195), indicating strong antagonistic
effects.
Through multiple linear regression and cross-validation, this study systematically
assessed the influence strength and stability of each variable on total P flow.
Sensitivity coefficient analysis indicated that core variables such as “Animal
products” and “Cereals” had the strongest explanatory power for total output, with
high robustness across different combinations. Interaction term analysis further

=== PDF PAGE 107 ===
106
revealed synergistic and antagonistic effects among variables, allowing the model to
capture the complex interdependencies within the industry chain. The high
consistency between training and test set R² and controllable prediction errors
demonstrate that the model results are reliable and stable, parameter identification is
accurate, and sensitivity coefficients are reasonable. These findings provide a
scientifically robust basis for P flow and trade predictions, supporting informed
decision-making.
10.7 Structural stability analysis of the contributions of key P flows in each
subsystem to total P flow growth
In this study, we conducted a structural analysis of interannual variations within
each subsystem. By examining the overall contribution of key P flows at different
stages of their evolution, we assessed whether the structural configuration of these
flows was reasonable and whether any abrupt changes occurred throughout the
long-term time series. This evaluation allowed us to verify the structural stability and
the result reliability of the P substance flow analysis model used in this study. As
shown in Figure S19, the P flows of all subsystems, including product structures,
cropland input structures, agricultural planting systems, livestock and poultry
production systems, household consumption systems, and waste management systems,
exhibited generally steady growth trends. The contribution structure of the major P
flows within each subsystem remained reasonable and consistent with the long-term P
flow pattern. Their contributions to P flow increases across different periods were
within a rational range, and no subsystem showed abrupt changes.

=== PDF PAGE 108 ===
107
For example, over the past half-century, the growth of P flows into cropland has
been dominated by chemical fertilizer inputs, accounting for approximately 72% to
78% of the total P flow, with a gradually declining trend. This pattern is consistent
with existing findings regarding the contribution of chemical fertilizers to cropland P
inputs. Although the contributions of animal manure and crop-straw organic fertilizers
were relatively small, their proportions remained stable over time. In the agricultural
planting system, crop products consistently accounted for the major output flow, at
about 66%, followed by straw and agricultural drainage. Contributions from grain
products and animal products were relatively balanced, although the share of animal
products increased over time. The marked increase in P flows embodied in animal
feed and poultry meat, along with the rising contribution from slaughter by-products,
reflected the growing demand for animal-based products, as shown in Fig. S7. This
trend is consistent with the global P flow patterns identified in our analysis. With the
shift in global dietary preferences, the share of P flows associated with crop-based
products has declined. In contrast, the share of animal-based foods has continued to
increase.
In the waste management system, although the total amount of recoverable P
increased significantly and the recycling rates of various waste streams improved, the
proportion of livestock and poultry manure returned to cropland, which is the most
critical component, showed a declining trend. This indicates an increasing decoupling
between livestock production systems and agricultural cropping systems at the global
scale, resulting in substantial P losses from livestock waste. It also highlights the

=== PDF PAGE 109 ===
108
considerable potential for P recovery and sustainable improvement through integrated
crop and livestock systems, as well as through cross-sectoral system management
strategies that incorporate the consumption system as revealed in our study. The P
recovery potential of livestock systems, including waste management systems, at both
global and national levels, is discussed in Section 4.2, and the sustainability potential
of crop – livestock integration and the comprehensive benefits of incorporating the
consumption system are discussed in Sections 4.3 and 4.4.
Text S11 Country-level P import–export patterns and the core
commodities driving these flows
The global P flow driven by trade in agricultural products has become a critical
aspect of the global anthropogenic P cycle. Total P flows through international
agricultural trade have increased nearly fourfold from 1990 (1.27 Mt) to 2022 (4.52
Mt), representing an eightfold increase since 1961 (Figure 2e-h in manuscript). In
1990, P flows from agricultural product trade accounted for only 8.07% of global P
fertilizer inputs to croplands (Figure 2e). By 2022, this figure had risen to 24.76%
(Figure 2h). The spatial heterogeneity of the bilateral P flows from traded agricultural
products facilitates long-distance P transfers and creates structural imbalances in
global P resources. Figure 2e-h illustrates the global agricultural product trade
network from 1990 to 2020, highlighting an increase in trading nations and a
significant expansion in trade volumes. North America emerged as a key P supplier
through agricultural exports to Asian countries, whereas South America made
substantial contributions to P inflows into Western Europe and Asia (Figure 2e-h).
Asia and Europe became major P sinks, accounting for 47.12% and 31.42% of global
P inputs (2022), respectively, with Asia relying on long-distance and globally sourced

=== PDF PAGE 110 ===
109
agricultural imports, while Europe's P flows were largely confined within the region
(Figure 2e-h).
The imbalance in the structure of P flows in international agricultural trade is
primarily driven by key countries. In total, 35 countries representing 6.5% of the
global agricultural product trade network dominate P imports and exports (Figure
S12). Over the past 50 years, exporting nations have contributed 121.33 Mt of
agricultural P, with net exporters including the United States (23.34%), Brazil
(9.83%), Argentina (8.56%), Canada (7.04%), France (5.57%), Australia (3.93%), and
Germany (3.57%) (Figures S9 and S12). Net agricultural P importers include densely
populated countries such as China (13.68 Mt) and Japan (7.45 Mt), as well as
livestock-intensive regions such as the Netherlands (6.39 Mt), Italy (4.31 Mt), and
Spain (3.65 Mt. Additionally, nations facing food security challenges, particularly
those surrounding the Mediterranean Basin, include Algeria, Morocco, Turkey, and
Egypt.
Global P flows through the crop product trade are driven by a core set of
commodities. These commodities are categorized into food, feed, and other uses, with
P flow patterns in the agricultural trade varying across regions (Figure S12). From
1961 to 2022, the exchange of cereals, soybeans, and feed (including feed cakes)
accounted for >80% of the total P flows through international trade, specifically
38.38%, 21.13%, and 20.94% of the total P flows, respectively, in 2022, totaling 3.64
Mt). In 2022, approximately 46.96% and 42.08% of P in traded agricultural products
were consumed as feed and food, respectively (Figure 2g in manuscript). Notably,
approximately 43% of the global cereal production was used for animal feed. P-flow
for all categories of P-containing products is listed in Table S7.

excreta from cropping systems. This scenario serves as a reference case relative to the scenario assuming 100% land application of human manure. Accordingly,
