Supporting Figures
Figure S1. A multi-model nested framework for studying the global anthropogenic P cycle. The framework
comprises six main components (Parts I–VI): (I) full-chain P-sector inventories, (II) a dynamic P substance
flow analysis (P_SFA) model based on production sectors, (III) a global PR depletion model, (IV) a
trade-driven global P interaction simulation, (V) a P footprint resource and environmental assessment model,
and (VI) a global P sustainability simulation. Components (I)–(IV) quantify mining, flow, long-distance
transfer, loss, and accumulation of global P resources. Part (V) provides a spatial visualization of global P
resource utilization and the environmental burdens associated with the production, consumption, and trade
sectors. Based on components (I–V), the sixth part (VI) conducts scenario simulations to explore the
sustainable utilization and management of global P under future demand conditions.“P loss” (pollution
footprint) is defined as the portion of P released from the socio-economic production and consumption
sectors into the environment that cannot be recovered. For example, P in cropland drainage or livestock
manure is not recycled back to agricultural land. P sustainability targets are defined as reductions in P inputs,
emissions, and environmental loading, along with improvements in P-use efficiency and recycling rates. A
detailed hierarchical description and modeling process of the coupled model can be found in Text S4. For
more detailed information on intersectoral P flows and P processes involving natural systems, see Figures
S2–S3. Pi, phosphate; AP, agricultural product; AD, atmospheric deposition. “–” denotes mitigation
measures, “+” denotes enhancement measures, “↔” denotes dietary shift measures, and the recycling symbol
denotes P recycling measures.

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
147
Figure S2. Static analytical model boundary of anthropogenic P cycles 8, 26. P 1PR: P phosphorite resources, P 2CI:
P chemical product production system, P 3A : Agricultural cropping system, P 4S : Stockbreeding system, P 5F :
Food processing system, P 6C : Residential consumption system, PAD: The amount of P through atmospheric
deposition, PSD: The amount of P deposited in the soil, PSNPK: The P content of returned straw, MWTP:
Municipal wastewater treatment plant. Numbers represent the order of subsystems. P losses mainly occur in
the following processes: tailings, waste residues, phosphogypsum, and surface runoff during phosphate
mining; waste residues and wastewater from the P chemical industry; agricultural runoff, soil erosion, and
leaching in crop production systems; livestock manure and slaughter waste in animal husbandry systems; and
food processing losses, food waste, and domestic wastewater in household consumption systems.

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===
148
Figure S3. System boundary of anthropogenic P_SFA model and the key P flow pathways. The pathways
were developed based on our team’s earlier P-SFA model 8, 26. The arrows describe the inputs and outputs of
the P flow. For example, PCI-A represents the flow from PCI to PA, symbol abbreviations are explained in
Figure S1 and Text S4, and the calculation process is described in Text S4. The boxes indicate individual
processes or sub-processes in which some human activities related to P flows occur. These sub-processes
may vary slightly in different ecosystems due to their various economic and consumption activities. Stock
refers to the accumulation of P in these subsystems, which does not flow among different activities in a short
time (at least one year). P 1PR : P phosphorite resources, P 2CI : P chemical product production system, P 3A :
Agricultural cropping system, P 4S : Stockbreeding system, P 5F : Food processing system, P 6C : Residential
consumption system, PAD: The amount of P through atmospheric deposition, PSD: The amount of P deposited
in the soil, PSNPK: The P content of returned straw, MWTP: Municipal wastewater treatment plant. Numbers
represent the order of subsystems. P losses mainly occur in the following processes: tailings, waste residues,
phosphogypsum, and surface runoff during phosphate mining; waste residues and wastewater from the P
chemical industry; agricultural runoff, soil erosion, and leaching in crop production systems; livestock
manure and slaughter waste in animal husbandry systems; and food processing losses, food waste, and
domestic wastewater in household consumption systems.

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===
149
Figure S4. Natural P cycling processes are closely associated with anthropogenic P flows. Natural P
processes are relatively complex, and this integrative schematic pathway is designed to provide readers with
a complete picture of P dynamics (both anthropogenic and natural), thereby making the research framework
more comprehensive. Considering that a large proportion of P flows within the socio-economic system occur
through agricultural cropping systems (Figure S2), and that natural processes are also closely linked to
cropland, we supplemented the P flows connecting the social and natural systems from the perspective of the
cropping system. Most natural P processes are quantified within the P_SFA model, including P loss
pathways such as atmospheric deposition, agricultural drainage, soil sedimentation, soil erosion, and leaching.
The calculation procedures are provided in Text S5 and in the P footprint analysis section (Text S7). The
abbreviations of the symbols are explained in Figure S2 and Text S4.

changes in phosphate rock resources for the world and core countries (i-Morocco, j-China,

conditions and existing technologies132. The effect of price on reserve availability is not
considered (Text S6); however, it should be noted that as prices increase, lower-grade deposits
also become economically feasible to exploit48.

Figure S12. Structural balance of P flow in global agricultural trade was dominated by 35 key countries and a set of
critical agricultural products from 1961 to 2021. The x-axis represents the export volumes above and the import volumes
below. These 35 countries can be categorized into exporter countries (a-j), importer countries (k-y), and trade-balanced
countries (z-ai), with the balance reflected in the total P exchange, although there are significant differences in the types of
products imported and exported.

=== PDF PAGE 160 ===
159
Figure S13. The pathway diagram of P footprint (P resource footprint and P pollution footprint)
driven by agricultural production and consumption. The P resource footprint and pollution
footprint primarily occur during local P production and consumption processes. The P resource
footprint, represented by inputs, includes mineral phosphate fertilizers, recycled organic P, as well
as product consumption and trade. In contrast, the P pollution footprint, represented by outputs,
corresponds to P losses as defined in Figure S1, which refer to the portion of P released from
socio-economic production and consumption sectors into the environment that cannot be
recovered. This is particularly evident in crop production systems, where P losses occur through
agricultural runoff, soil deposition, erosion, and leaching. Notably, trade leads to the transfer and
redistribution of the P resource footprint; however, the P pollution footprint generated during the
production of P-containing goods remains localized in the producing regions. This constitutes one
of the key drivers of the imbalance in global P flows induced by trade.

=== PDF PAGE 161 ===
160
Figure S14. The national-level net P budget related to agricultural production and consumption from 1961 to 2022. CPF: chemical phosphate fertilizer.

=== PDF PAGE 162 ===
161
Figure S15. Global cropland erosion-runoff-leaching fraction (β).
Figure S16. Comparison of P substitutability index of different countries. According to the
research methods of Rahman et al. 131, a comprehensive assessment of P recycling at the national
level was conducted, providing the substitutability index for different countries. The P
substitutability index they defined is the amount of P recovered in a country divided by the total P
entering the system, indicating the percentage of P imports that can be substituted through
recycling practices (P recycling potential). Considering the significant role of agricultural
production in the P cycle framework, this study used the amount of P entering the agricultural
system as the total inflow. Countries or regions with an index of 1 were considered not to be taken
into account due to a lack of arable land or insufficient arable area.

baseline under integrated measures and scenarios. NPR, New P demand, representing P entering
the social system from P mines; CFR, chemical fertilizer demand; IFD, industrial feed demand;
FD, forage demand; FGD, feed grain demand; AFD, aquaculture feed demand; PMS, phosphate
mining system; CIS, chemical industry system; ACS, agricultural cropping system; LBS, livestock

treatment system; CL, cultivated land; NCL, non-cultivated land; SWS, surface water system; PPS,

=== PDF PAGE 164 ===

Figure S18. Interdepartmental potential for enhancing P use and recycling to reduce reliance on
PR and achieve resource sustainability. ① and ② represent primary and secondary P, respectively.
All measures focused on quantifying the P sustainability potential within the agricultural sector,
with the meaning of P benefits varying across sectors. Specifically, the value for the phosphate
mining sector represents the reduction rate in future phosphate production; the livestock sector
value reflects the efficiency gains in P utilization; the waste sector value indicates the benefits of P
recovery; the crop production sector value corresponds to the reduction rate in P demand; and the
environmental sector value represents the reduction rate in P emissions. The value in the title of
each sector indicates the optimization benefits under comprehensive measures. MPF, mineral
phosphate fertilizer. A supplementary explanation of the future sustainable P management
pathways from a global production system perspective can be found in Text S9.

=== PDF PAGE 165 ===

Figure S19. Phase-specific changes in P flows across subsystems and structural stability analysis
of key P-flow contributions to total P-flow growth for evaluating the reliability of the P_SFA. All
product structures (a), cropland input structures (b), agricultural planting systems (c), livestock
and poultry production systems (d), household consumption systems (e), and waste management
systems (f) exhibit generally steady growth trends in P flows. The structures of major P flows
within each subsystem remain reasonable and consistent with the long-term P flow patterns. The
contributions of key flows to P flow increases across different periods fall within a rational range,
and no abrupt changes in P flows are observed in any subsystem.

Figure S20. Uncertainty analysis results of key P flows in each subsystem. Each column represents a different subsystem, with the same color used within
each subsystem for clarity. In panel (i), positive values represent straw used as organic fertilizer, while negative values represent straw used as feed; in panel
(t), positive values indicate feed exports from aquaculture, while negative values indicate feed imports. Given the complexity of P flow pathways and the
need for effective data visualization, we present the uncertainty analysis results for the key subsystems and significant P flows. In the model calculations,
uncertainty in parameters was considered for all P flow estimations, unless the underlying data or empirical coefficients were limited in source or accuracy.
